#7155. Senior Deliverability and Compliance Specialist at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Most Kit creators don't have a dedicated Creator Growth Manager, and when their emails start landing in spam, they need someone who can find the cause quickly and explain it in plain language. We're looking for a Deliverability and Compliance Specialist to own deliverability support for that long tail of creators, run Kit's abuse desk, and make the compliance calls that protect the sending reputation every creator on Kit shares.

You'll also build the playbooks, training, and AI-assisted workflows that help Support and contractors handle more deliverability questions on their own, and you'll partner with our infrastructure engineers as we run more of our sending in-house. A creator can't build a lasting business if their emails don't arrive, so this work connects directly to our mission.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes digging through headers and bounce logs to find the real cause of a problem, can explain DMARC alignment to a first-time creator without jargon, and is comfortable making a firm call when a situation is gray. You don't wait for someone to tell you what needs fixing. You proactively spot opportunities, identify patterns, and take the initiative to improve deliverability before problems arise.

You'll report to Alyssa Dulin, Director of Creator Success, and collaborate closely with:

Our Deliverability team: a Deliverability Lead who supports our highest-value accounts and leads deliverability education, and a Deliverability Specialist who onboards and ramps up large senders moving to Kit

Our infrastructure engineers, who run Kit's sending infrastructure

Our compliance and anti-abuse engineers, who build the automated checks that keep spammers off Kit

Product Specialists and the Support team, who handle the front line and escalate deliverability issues to you

Customer Success Managers (we call them Creator Growth Managers at Kit), Sales and Migrations, and Product Managers

First Week: Get set up in Kit, our admin tools, and our support and ticketing systems, and meet our distributed team in Get To Know You chats (GTKYs). Meet the deliverability team and get an introduction on the current projects. Read our deliverability playbooks and SOPs, and get access to the monitoring tools you'll use every day: Google Postmaster Tools, Microsoft SNDS, blocklist monitoring, and our internal delivery dashboards.

First Month: Shadow the Deliverability team and Product Specialists on deliverability tickets, account approvals, and abuse reports, then start answering queue tickets with review. Join the daily monitoring rotation (blocklist delisting, Postmaster and delivery monitoring) and the weekly Deliverability team meeting. Learn how account approvals, compliance flags, and our in-house sending platform work. By the end of the month, work deliverability tickets and abuse reports on your own and send your first delisting requests. Project: build a recurring report showing the trending deliverability ticket reasons so we understand what’s causing how to reduce friction for customers and how to solve it

First Six Months: Own the deliverability support queue for creators without a dedicated CGM: spam-folder placement, bounces, authentication and DMARC setup, and escalations from Product Specialists. Own compliance end to end, from new-account approvals and compliance flags to pausing or closing accounts that put our shared reputation at risk.Run Kit's abuse desk. You'll write and share a strategy that covers coverage, response-time targets, escalation paths, and quality checks, then put it in place so routine spam, phishing, and blocklist reports follow clear SOPs while you take the hardest cases yourself. Be the go-to contact for day-to-day mailbox provider issues with Microsoft, Gmail, Yahoo, Comcast, and regional providers, and write an incident runbook for blocklistings and provider throttling. Track the queue with a simple scorecard (volume, first response time, and escalation rate) and share what you learn with the team. Run training sessions for Support and ship your first process or AI improvements, like cleaner ticket categorization or better account data for our AI support agent, so more deliverability tickets get solved without escalation..

First Year: Deliverability support is faster and more consistent for every creator on Kit, including the ones who never talk to a CGM. Your playbooks, help center articles, and AI-assisted workflows are what Support, CGMs, and contractors reach for first, and fewer tickets need to come to you. You've led at least one cross-team fix with infrastructure for a recurring problem you spotted in the queue, such as an alert when a creator's domain authentication breaks or a check for sudden signup spikes. You're helping shape the internal tooling and alerting our in-house sending needs, and you take part in industry groups like M3AAWG.

Deep working knowledge of email authentication (SPF, DKIM, DMARC, alignment, and BIMI basics), plus the ability to read raw message headers and SMTP bounce and deferral codes

Hands-on use of Google Postmaster Tools and Microsoft SNDS/JMRP, and comfort working with major blocklists (Spamhaus, Abusix, Barracuda, Spamcop, and others) and their delisting processes

Data fluency: you can pull bounce, complaint, and engagement data together, find the root cause, and explain it so others can act on it

Hands-on abuse handling: you can work an abuse report or escalation start to finish, from finding the account and assessing the evidence to acting, replying to the reporter, and documenting the decision

Process building: you can turn repeat work into SOPs, playbooks, and quality checks that a contractor or teammate can follow

Clear, calm written communication with creators, teammates, and postmasters

Using AI tools to research, summarize, and draft, and helping teammates do the same, with the judgment to keep a person on final compliance calls

Experience with MTA logs or delivery observability tools (KumoMTA, PowerMTA, Postfix, Kibana/Elasticsearch, or SQL and BI tools like Mode)

Familiarity with feedback loops, spam traps, list bombing, and bot-signup patterns

Familiarity with CAN-SPAM, CASL, and GDPR as they apply to marketing email

Experience with Intercom, HubSpot, or a similar ticketing tool

Experience building or tuning AI support workflows or macros

4+ years in email deliverability, compliance, trust and safety, or technical support at an ESP, mailbox provider, or high-volume sender

Handling escalated customer tickets end to end, including with customers who are frustrated

Making account-level compliance or abuse decisions, such as approving, suspending, or closing accounts

Working an abuse desk or abuse mailbox directly: spam and phishing complaints, provider and blocklist reports, and escalations

Leading the fix for a recurring deliverability or compliance problem that needed help from more than one team

Supporting senders on shared IP pools at an ESP

Setting abuse or trust and safety strategy, or directing contractors or vendors on a support or abuse queue

Working directly with mailbox provider postmaster teams

Participating in M3AAWG or other industry groups

Creating educational content, like help docs, training, workshops, or podcasts

Working with creators, digital entrepreneurs, or small businesses

You pick up problems that fall between teams without waiting for permission, and you follow them through until they're closed.

You test a hypothesis against the evidence before you act, and you frame problems clearly enough that others trust your analysis.

You protect the reputation every creator shares while staying fair and kind to the creator in front of you, and you help them understand the reasoning behind a decision.

You turn what you learn into docs, templates, and training that others use, and you adjust how you teach to fit the audience.

You hold a high bar for your work, and you push back respectfully, with data, when something puts our reputation at risk, even when Sales or leadership would prefer a different answer.

You share in-progress work and risks early so the team can solve problems together.

Treating deliverability as a set of macros rather than an investigation that needs judgment

Discomfort saying no to a creator or a teammate when our shared reputation is at stake

Waiting for someone to assign you work when you can already see the problem

Needing a fixed playbook, since our sending stack and processes are changing as we bring more sending in-house

Keeping knowledge in your head instead of writing it down for the next person

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe. We work mostly on East Coast time - so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. We do our best to keep things reasonable, but a little time zone juggling comes with the territory.

#7152. ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Planning Huge Rule-Change Next Season to Stop These Celebs From Being Voted Off

Dancing With the Stars has officially wrapped season 35, and the show is already preparing for season 36 next fall. And apparently producers are making a huge change to the voting process to prevent high-scoring couples (think: Jenna Dewan and Val Chmerkovskiy) from being eliminated too early in the competition.

“Producers have already discussed bringing back the judges’ save for next season. Again, it’s still early but that is more than likely going to be brought back,” a source tells The Sun. “They’ve already made it clear they want to shake it up next season, drastically.”

The judges’ save (which was previously in effect from Season 28 to Season 32) lets the judges step in and decide which of the bottom two pairings goes home after fans have already voted. Ultimately, it’s a way to counter fan favoritism overriding high scores.

DWTS abandoned the judges’ save because it ultimately gave the experts more power than viewers at home. As executive producer Deena Katz told TV Line, “I think it should be about America. And at the end of the day, the judges' save sometimes isn't what America wanted. And [eliminations are based on] half the judges and half America voting, and so that's kind of the reason we did it.”

FYI, Dancing With the Stars’ UK version, Strictly Come Dancing, still uses the judges’ save, and judge Shirley Ballas explained to Extra, “I think it's good that the audience choose. They put two in the bottom. That was their choice. And then, at the end of the day, we get to save the dancer that we think is the best. Because sometimes, on your kind of show... they could send all the good dancers home. And then, you know, you're left with a different final. So it's important that there's something for everybody.”

#7151. Senior Analytics Engineer at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. Kit is growing, so we're looking for a Senior Analytics Engineer to own Kit's sales and commerce data from end to end.

Accurate sales data gives our team a clear picture of the pipeline and the confidence to make informed decisions. That’s what makes this role so important.

Your primary focus will be Sales: the dbt models that turn our CRM data into trusted pipeline, deal, and funnel metrics, and the Omni dashboards our sales leader and Senior Revenue Operations Manager use to run the business. You'll also model creator commerce data, so we understand what creators sell and earn through Kit, and you'll pick up reporting work with our Product team, such as feature usage reporting. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. When Sales and Product have clear, reliable data, we help more creators build more valuable businesses with Kit.

You'll report to Sam Umachi, Head of Data (Linkedin).

Sales and RevOps are your primary partners, who will rely on the models and reporting you own. You'll also partner with our Product team on reporting for features like Visual Automations and on how creators use Kit.

Within our data team, you'll work alongside a Staff Analytics Engineer who owns org-wide semantic layer governance and modeling standards, a Senior Data Engineer who owns ingestion and pipelines, and a Senior Business Analytics Partner focused on revenue opportunities. This role has no direct reports.

First Week: Work through our onboarding curriculum in Notion and meet the data team, our Senior Revenue Operations Manager, the sales team, and your product partners. Get set up in our core tools: dbt Cloud, GitHub, Amazon Redshift, Omni, HubSpot, Linear, Hightouch and Slack. Set up your AI-assisted development environment and learn the shared AI workflows our data team uses. Open and merge your first small pull request so you learn our review and deployment flow early.

First Month: Map how HubSpot objects (deals, pipelines and stages, companies, contacts, and owners) flow into our dbt models, and document the gaps. Take over day-to-day ownership of the sales models and Omni dashboards that teammates have been covering. Audit current sales reporting and list where definitions differ or numbers don't reconcile. Get familiar with how commerce data (products creators sell and the revenue they earn through Kit) lands in the warehouse today. Agree with RevOps and Sales on the most important questions they need answered. Review teammates' pull requests and contribute to our shared SQL, dbt, and documentation standards. Share a written reflection with the team on what you've learned and where you'd focus first.

First Six Months: Ship and maintain well-tested, documented dbt models for core sales metrics, such as pipeline, deal velocity, stage conversion, win rates, and the sales-led funnel. Expose those metrics through the Omni semantic layer, working within the team's governance standards. Rebuild or refresh the core sales dashboards so pipeline reviews and forecasting conversations run on numbers that don't need manual reconciliation. Resolve competing sales metric definitions by writing down the options and tradeoffs, aligning with Sales and RevOps, and documenting the outcome. Model creator-level commerce data, such as products sold, revenue earned through Kit, and conversion, in partnership with data engineering. Write semantic layer descriptions, metric definitions, and documentation that let Omni's AI and MCP-connected AI tools answer routine sales questions correctly, and test them against real questions from Sales, RevOps and Product. Get Sales to the point where routine questions are answered through self-serve, whether in Omni dashboards, Omni's AI, or AI tools connected over MCP, rather than one-off requests.

First Year: Be the recognized owner of Kit's sales data, the person Sales and RevOps go to first and trust without second-guessing. Give our revenue analysis work the sales and commerce models it depends on, so deeper questions about pipeline health and creator earnings can be answered quickly, thereby enabling us to make creators more successful. Leave your domains well documented and well tested, legible to new teammates, stakeholders, and the AI agents that query our semantic layer. Help raise the team's modeling quality through code review, pairing, and sharing what you learn.

Deep dbt experience. You build modular, tested, documented dbt projects, and you're comfortable with incremental models, snapshots, macros, CI, and scheduled jobs in dbt Cloud or similar.

Advanced SQL. You reason carefully about grain, joins, and window functions, and you write queries that are readable and performant on a cloud warehouse.

Data modeling for sales and GTM. You can model deals, pipeline stages and their history, ownership changes, and funnel conversion into clean, reusable structures. You are opinionated on Kimball standards and when a concept should be a fact or dimension.

CRM data fluency. You know how sales data behaves in a CRM, including stage changes, backdated edits, and custom properties. HubSpot is preferred, but other CRM experience (e.g. Salesforce) applies just as well. We care most that you recognize common CRM patterns.

AI-assisted development. You use AI code editors and agents (such as Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, or similar) every day, and you validate their output with tests, query results, and review before anything ships.

Clear written communication. You explain metric definitions, tradeoffs, and data caveats in plain language that Sales, RevOps, and Product partners understand.

BI and semantic layer experience. You can build dashboards and semantic models in Omni, Looker, or a similar BI tool, and you design metric definitions that people and AI tools can both query correctly.

Git-based workflows. You keep pull requests small and well described, and you give thoughtful code review.

Nice to have: Redshift performance tuning (sort and distribution keys), Python for scripting or API pulls, reverse ETL, and experience with product event, billing, payments, or ecommerce data.

Around 7 or more years in analytics engineering or data engineering where you personally shipped production data models.

Several years of hands-on work building and maintaining production dbt projects.

Working directly with sales teams and RevOps, and owning reporting they relied on to run the business.

Owning a data domain over time and evolving its models as the business and its questions changed.

Working at a SaaS company, ideally one with both a self-serve and a sales-led motion. Commerce, payments, or creator economy experience is a plus, not a requirement.

Working on a small or remote team where you were trusted to make decisions independently.

Ownership and follow-through. You treat your data domains as yours. You notice problems before stakeholders do, close the loop on requests, and finish what you start.

Judgment. You match rigor to the decision at hand, know when a quick answer is enough and when a request should become a durable model, and say so clearly.

Flexibility. You're glad to step outside your primary area when another team needs help, and you balance that work against your core commitments openly.

Teach everything you know. You document as you go, pair generously, and help stakeholders understand the data well enough to use it on their own.

Work in public. You share progress, open questions, and decisions in writing where the team can see them, including what didn't work.

Creator success is our success. You remember who's on the other end. A wrong number in a pipeline review means Sales spends time on the wrong creators, and that shapes how carefully you work.

Do it with excellence. You test your work, review it carefully, and don't ship a number you can't defend.

Candor with care. You're comfortable pushing back when a request won't answer the real question, and you do it in a way that builds trust.

Needing detailed specs or a highly structured environment before you can start.

Discomfort with ambiguity. Some of our sales and commerce definitions are still evolving, and you'll help settle them.

Needing several layers of approval to make modeling decisions within your domain.

Wanting to work only on dashboards, or only on pipelines, rather than across the transformation and BI layers.

Wanting a strictly fixed scope. Sales comes first, but you'll pick up Product or other work when the business needs it.

Wanting mostly greenfield work. Much of this role is improving sales models and dashboards that already exist.

Preferring to take tickets as written rather than partnering with stakeholders on what they need.

Being skeptical of AI's capabilities, or using AI without good judgment and accountability.

Expecting a large data team with dedicated specialists for every part of the stack.

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe. We work mostly on East Coast time - so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. We do our best to keep things reasonable, but a little time zone juggling comes with the territory.

Kit has standardized salaries based on position, no matter where you live. For this role, we're hiring at our level 4 ($172,000). Level is determined based on experience and our interview process.

We cover up to $2,280 per month toward medical premiums, with dental and vision premiums fully covered. We offer Health Insurance plans through Aetna

$2,000 equipment allowance for your first two years, $1,000 budget every following two years. Company-provided laptops are issued to every Kit team member and are not included in the equipment budget

Individual learning + development budget ($3,500/year)

Creator meetup budget ($50/month) to connect with creators over coffee, lunch, or other in-person meetups

Childcare benefit up to $3,000 annually through JOON

Twenty (20) days of paid time off during each year of employment

Paid paid vacation: An after-tax bonus of $1,000 for taking five consecutive days of vacation where you’re fully unplugged from work

Two weeks of paid sick time each year, including mental health + well being days

Twelve (12) weeks paid parental leave and flexible scheduling in your child’s first year

Up to six weeks of paid bereavement leave, medical leave, and disaster after six months of employment, two weeks of each paid leave in your first six months

Winter Break Closure: Kit closes for a week at the end of December, giving everyone a collective break to enjoy the holiday season. Essential support services remain available, with teams coordinating to ensure coverage during this period

Four-week, paid sabbatical after five years with the team

Fantastic in-person or virtual retreats with the team twice a year

#7148. Getting Ready With Jared McCain

The chronically online NBA player’s routine is all about preserving his mental health. And yes, he has read your comments about his nails.

Jared McCain loves a routine—several routines, actually, which is why there’s a whiteboard in his bathroom, covered with a bulleted list of his daily to-dos (journaling, affirmations, bone broth, and lymphatic jumps, among them). That’s all separate from the 22-year-old’s bedtime and wake-up regimens (silk eye mask, silk pillowcase, silk bonnet, magnesium, and mouth tape, then a multistep skincare process, respectively). And this doesn’t even include many of the things his 7.7 million social media followers have come to love: Jared hanging out in de-puffing eye patches. Jared wandering around wearing Gen Z’s favorite pimple patches. Jared flashing his latest bright-colored manicure.

As he moves through his Oklahoma City home, where he lives with three of his best friends, it’s clear that none of the above is purely performative, as is so often the case with internet celebrities. “I do a lot of skincare because I sweat a lot,” he says as he works his way through what looks like the entire Hailey Bieber–founded Rhode skincare line. “I really like to take care of my face.”

Click through the videos on his TikTok or Instagram page and you’ll likely see that face illuminated by LED lights. “One of my favorite parts about my house is my red light showerhead,” he says. “Red light just creates this vibe and gives me health benefits. It’s number one in my life.” It’s also on his whiteboard three times.

Jared first broke into virality as a starting guard on Duke University’s basketball team. And while his talent on the court would lead him to the NBA—he now plays for the Oklahoma City Thunder—it was something else that captured the wider world’s attention. “I started painting my nails during COVID, and that’s when I also started on TikTok,” he explains. “I played with the polish on and I had a really good game, so I thought, Hold on, this might be the new thing.” (He now has brand partnerships with Sally Hansen and Ulta Beauty.)

“I didn’t really care what other people thought about it,” he says of any negative feedback he got at the time. He now keeps manicure inspiration cataloged on a Pinterest board—“most of the time, I’m feeling colorful; I love bright, bright colors”—and his Oklahoma City–based nail artist Yvie McCrary often sends him ideas to prep for his next appointment.

“In the NBA, there can be a lot of scrutiny, so I try to take care of my mental health,” he adds. In his downstairs “energy room,” he flicks on an LED panel (of course) for his daily yoga flow. “I love finding peace,” he says. “Meditation allows me to assess what’s going on in my life.” It’s yet another habit he uses to work through any unfavorable comments or haters he comes across: “It’s funny at this point. I’ve gotten used to it by now. It’s a blessing to even be talked about.”

Not that everything Jared does is 100 percent zen. When he’s not indulging in self-betterment practices, he’s posting TikTok dances and lip syncs from the Thunder’s locker room, sometimes with special appearances from his teammates.

“Entertaining has always been so fun to me,” he says. “That’s kind of what basketball is, too. Growing up, I thought I was going to be on the Disney Channel or a pop star. I always end up singing some Olivia Dean.” His plea for the singer to bring him onstage during her Art of Loving Tour stop at Madison Square Garden in New York City got nearly 2 million views (sadly, she did not invite him up onstage).

He launches into a rendition of Olivia’s “A Couple Minutes” with surprisingly impressive vocals. “The song could make me cry right now,” he confesses. One of his roommates later jokes, “We need a mute button”—something Jared’s fans have never asked for and likely never will.

Director of video: Sylvie Flanagan. Director/senior producer: Sarah Pusateri. Production assistant: Nate Cody. DP: Nicholas Moo. Camera: Maggie Rimassa. Editor: Theresa Shea. Sound: Grant Provence.

Jasmine Hyman is the Assistant Beauty Editor at Cosmopolitan, where she writes about the latest beauty trends and must-have products. Her most prized beauty possessions are a meticulous skincare routine and salon blowouts. You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

Dancing With the Stars just aired one of its best finales everrrrr, resulting in Ezra Frech and Daniella Karagach being crowned winners! And apparently this was a best-case scenario for producers, who—per an insider—were hoping Ezra would win.

“Producers are mildly panicking because in a best case scenario world, Ezra would win,” a source told The Sun ahead of the live finale on Tuesday night. “He is in producers’ mind the perfect representation of a winner for this season.”

Per the outlet, producers believe Ezra could be the show’s “golden boy,” but were worried that his live scores wouldn’t be high enough to net a win after noticing engagement on his social media had “dropped significantly.” But clearly fans came out in droves voting for Ezra and Daniella, which—combined with their perfect scores all night—resulted in them winning!

In case you missed it, Ezra reflected on his beautiful freestyle routine in the skybox, and talked about his prosthetic unexpectedly coming off mid-performance. “It’s crazy. You do something so many times,” he said. “The leg un-suctioned, and it came off in the middle of the dance. But listen, we put our heart, our blood, our sweat, our tears into this. If one person with a disability feels more confident, holds their head up high because of this show, then I’ve done my job.”

#7128. Amber Glenn Reveals She Was Drug Tested Ahead of ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Finale

“Third time they’ve tested me this season, by the way.”

Amber Glenn and Pasha Pashkov came in second during an absolutely incredible Dancing With the Stars finale, and their flawless freestyle is even more impressive knowing how little sleep Amber’s running on. The Olympian told Variety that she was drug tested the night before the finale, saying, “I actually had...you saw the drug testing show up last night. Yeah, so didn’t get much sleep. Third time they’ve tested me this season, by the way, guys, what’s happening? So, I’m excited to get some rest.”

At this point you’re probably wondering if Dancing With the Stars is drug testing contestants, which, nope! As Variety notes in their Twitter caption, Amber is referring to being tested by USADA (United States Anti-Doping Agency), which is the “national anti-doping organization (NADO) in the United States for Olympic, Paralympic, Pan American, and Parapan American sport.”

As their website puts it, they’re “charged with managing the anti-doping program, including in-competition and out-of-competition testing, results management processes, drug reference resources, and athlete education for all United States Olympic & Paralympic Committee (USOPC) recognized sport national governing bodies, their athletes, and events.”

Since Amber is currently training for the U.S. Figure Skating Championships (taking place in Utah this January), she’s being tested by the USADA like all athletes. Which might come as a surprise to everyone who didn’t realize Amber’s been juggling training with DWTS!

Her coach, Damon Allen, explained to KRDO “She's been balancing dance on a rigorous schedule with that, as well as, being able to continue training, getting ready for nationals in January. It's been tiring. But she knows how to recover, and she's got a regimen, and she knows how to take care of her body, and figure out how to be at the top of her game at all times, whether it's in the dance arena or back on the ice.”

#7126. Product Manager - Postgres at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're hiring a Product Manager to own Postgres itself at Supabase. Every Supabase project is a full Postgres database, and Postgres is the foundation that Auth, Storage, Realtime and Edge Functions run on. You'll own the product experience of the database: Postgres versions and upgrades, maintenance, observability, extensions and security.

You'll work closely with our Postgres engineering teams and with the PM lead for Databases, who leads Multigres. You'll define the Postgres requirements that architecture must meet, and own how customers experience their database through that transition.

This role is ideal for someone who loves Postgres, sweats the details that make a database trustworthy, and is excited about improving the experience for millions of developers. Small improvements here compound across every project on the platform.

You'll own Postgres versions, upgrades and end-of-life. Which major and minor versions we support, how and when customers upgrade, and how we retire old versions. Your goal is to make upgrades routine rather than risky.

You'll make maintenance predictable. You'll design how we schedule, communicate and run maintenance windows so customers stay informed and in control, and disruption is rare and expected.

You'll help developers understand their database. Query performance, metrics, logs and advisors: you'll own the path from "my database is slow" to the fix.

You'll own our extension ecosystem. Which extensions we offer, how we evaluate and support new ones, and how we keep them working across versions.

You'll own Postgres security. Roles and permissions, Row Level Security ergonomics, secure defaults, and how we respond to Postgres and extension CVEs.

You'll talk to developers across the full spectrum, from indie developers on their first project to enterprises running critical workloads, and bring what you learn into every prioritization call.

You'll own the problem definition, requirements and success metrics for what we ship, such as upgrade success rate, the share of the fleet on supported versions, and time-to-diagnose for performance problems. You'll set the metric before the team builds, then track it after launch.

You'll keep Engineering, Support and GTM aligned. You'll own the plan of record for what's coming, why, and in what order, and communicate version changes and deprecations clearly to customers.

4+ years of Product Management experience on databases, developer tools or infrastructure, or equivalent depth as an engineer, DBA or founder moving into product. You're comfortable commenting on an architecture doc and advocating for the user when trade-offs are required.

Know Postgres well, or another relational database well enough to ramp quickly. You're comfortable reading EXPLAIN output and talking about replication, WAL, roles and extensions.

Have shipped products where reliability and trust are the experience. You treat downtime and breaking changes as product problems, not just operational ones.

Write clearly. Your specs, announcements and deprecation notices leave little room for confusion.

Build with what you own. You run queries, test upgrades and try extensions yourself, and form opinions through first-hand use.

Use AI to move faster. You use AI tools to compress research, drafting and feedback synthesis so you spend more time on judgment calls.

Work async by default. Supabase is fully remote. You make decisions in writing and move work forward without meetings.

Ran Postgres in production: upgrades, tuning, incident response.

Worked on database observability, performance tooling, or security and compliance products.

Contributed to Postgres, its extensions, or the wider open-source community.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

#7125. Texas Democrats propose bills to nix state DEI ban at public colleges

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The party clinched the state’s House and governorship in the midterm elections, though that still may not be enough to get the legislation passed.

In the state’s House, Reps. Nicole Collier and John Bucy III have put forward bills that would repeal the 2023 law. Texas state Sen. José Menéndez introduced a companion bill in the state’s Senate.

At any other time in the past three decades, the new bills might have faced little chance of passage simply by virtue of being championed by Democrats. But the party now controls two-thirds of a trifecta for the first time since 1996 — a makeup that may still not be enough to pass the legislation.

Senate Bill 17 — the target of the new bills — bans several DEI activities at public colleges, including:

Texas was one of the first states to ban DEI offices and initiatives in higher education. Following SB 17 and a similar law in Florida, universities from Ohio to Iowa to Alaska shuttered their DEI offices, often under legislative mandates.

Institutions quickly responded to SB 17. For instance, the University of North Texas said before the law took effect that it would restructure its Multicultural Center and Pride Alliance into the student affairs office and launch a new Center for Belonging and Engagement.

After passage, the Texas A&M University System issued guidance stating that DEI terminology couldn’t be used on the institution’s websites. Offices dedicated to student affinity communities would have to close, though it said some programs and libraries could be absorbed into broader offices and initiatives.

Texas Tech University also closed its DEI office.

Texas Tech University System’s latest chancellor — Brandon Creighton,  who helped author SB 17 — has gone even further than the bill’s requirements, which don’t cover academic content. The system has moved to eliminate academic programs focused on sexual orientation and gender identity and put restrictions on teaching about race, sex and gender in courses.

State audits so far have determined that the University of Texas System, nearly all of the institutions within theTexas A&M University System and 19 of the state’s community colleges are compliant with SB 17.

Audits last year deemed two institutions noncompliant — a community college because it made a new employee participate in a DEI training and Texas A&M-Central Texas because it struck a contract that didn’t conform to the law’s ban.

Senior leaders attributed its financial woes to “shifting enrollment trends, unpredictable state and federal funding, and rising costs.”

The congressional chamber could vote this week on the Protect College Sports Act, a proposal intended to stabilize the shifting collegiate landscape.

#7121. Education Department faces management challenges from recent changes, watchdog says

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An annual federal report cited interagency agreements and a handful of executive orders as key factors that will challenge the agency in 2027.

Since early 2025, the second Trump administration has outsourced many of the Education Department’s core functions such as grant management, monitoring and technical assistance to other agencies through interagency agreements. The department has also reorganized many of its offices and laid off a bulk of its staff.

"Change of this magnitude can result in challenges associated with employee resistance, fatigue, and uncertainty — such as decreased productivity, operational disruption, and lost efficiency — and have an impact on the organization’s ability to respond to stakeholder questions and concerns," the Nov. 13 report from OIG stated.

The Education Department has not yet issued an annual performance plan for fiscal year 2026 and has yet to issue its annual performance report for FY 2025, strategic plan for FY 2026-2030, or annual performance plan for FY 2027, according to the report.

Other reports through various federal offices have flagged similar concerns with the Education Department's output amid McMahon's efforts to close the agency, which ultimately requires Congressional approval.

In June, another OIG report found that layoffs at the Education Department appear to have impacted units that were performing legal duties.

The staffing changes were made to 16 of the department's 17 offices, resulting in an overall reduction of at least 40% of the more than 4,000 employees who were working for the Education Department when Trump reentered office in January 2025.

Many lawmakers, civil rights groups and lawyers have raised concerns with output from at least one of those offices, the Office for Civil Rights, which saw a slowdown in case resolutions that included no sexual harassment or assault cases being settled in 2025. A later report from the office of Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., showed that OCR resolved only 1% of its cases overall in 2025, the lowest percentage from any OCR in over the last decade.

Responding to a draft of the most recent OIG report, the Education Department said it had made progress in many of the areas of concern, including change management. According to OIG, the department said it has "continued efforts to improve operational processes and maintain program delivery during a period of substantial organizational change." Additionally, the agency said it has "engaged in contractor support to assist with resolution for external audits," among other adjustments.

The department has previously maintained that its staffing and organizational changes will not affect the statutory functions it is legally obliged to perform.

The department also stressed in a comment to K-12 Dive that the OIG report is an annual update. In a January OIG report, the office flagged similar oversight challenges stemming from executive orders and resulting organizational changes.

"It is critical that the Department strategically plan for and effectively manage these and other major operational changes that it may decide to undertake," it warned at the time.

Past reports have flagged challenges under previous administrations, such as monitoring grantees' use of COVID-19 pandemic relief funding.

#7119. How to Do a Chrome Manicure Over Regular Nail Polish If You’re Feeling Really Brave

This isn’t for the faint of heart, but it *can* be done.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

There are, in my view, four types of DIY nail art: Manicures that are as easy to do as they look, designs looks complicated but are actually pretty simple, motifs that are exactly as hard as they look, and nail art that seems like it should be a breeze but are actually a pain in the butt. Chrome manicures fall into that last category, especially if you’re trying to layer the metallic powder over regular polish.

In fact, chrome powder paired with regular polish is so finicky that some experts feel like it’s not worth the hassle to even try. “In my experience, improvising with loose chrome pigment over regular polish can become a long, tedious process with inconsistent results,” says nail artist Max Estrada. “A manicure should be a little luxury, not an endurance examination.”

It’s a fair warning: Chrome definitely works best with gel, and it’s going to take a lot of trial and error to get that mirror-like shine on top of regular polish. But there are lots of legit reasons that some people avoid gel in their at-home manicure station (allergies... you have to buy a whole UV setup... it’s tedious to remove...), and those people might still want to try the chocolate chrome look so beloved by Hailey Bieber right now. (Also, some of us are just artistic masochists who find it reasonable that the cost of creative satisfaction would be some failed attempts and frustrated tears.)

So, if you’re feeling up for the challenge—and have a lot of time to wait for your polish to dry completely—here are some tips for getting a chrome manicure with regular polish. (Results absolutely not guaranteed!)

While you can use any regular polish you’d like as your base color for this, there are a few different techniques that can work for a chrome finish, says Jackie of @jackie.shades. However, she’s gotten so good at applying chrome over regular polish people on the internet are calling her approach “The Jackie Method,” so I’m going to stick with that. For that method, you’ll need:

Chrome is not very forgiving of lumps and bumps, so you’ll need to do some careful nail prep before you get started. In addition to the usual filing, hangnail trimming, and cuticle pushing, Jackie recommends a smoothing base coat like Halo Taco Smoothing Base or What’s Up Nails Hideaway Ridge to give you a smooth base to start on.

As far as your station goes, Jackie emphasizes that chrome is messy. “Make sure you turn off any fans, work on a silicone mat or paper towel, try not to flick any dust into the air, and wear a face mask,” she says. That last point is because chrome powder is a super fine dust—you don’t want to breathe that in.

This is the method Jackie has found works best for her nails (and others on Instagram who’ve tried it say it works great for them too) but she leads with a big disclaimer that everyone is different. “Some products work for some and not for others,” Jackie says. “If something doesn’t work out, just keep experimenting.”

After your nails are prepped, apply thin layers of your color polish of choice. Keeping them thin is important, Jackie says, since this prevents air bubbles that will be noticeable once you add the chrome powder. Also, thin layers dry more quickly.

Having completely dry polish is one of the biggest keys to success for this look. “The base polish should be dry enough that if you press on your nail plate with your finger, it doesn’t leave any dents,” Jackie says. In my experience, this can take hours with some polish (I told you this wasn’t some easy breezy DIY mani). If you want to be really safe, you could even paint the base color on the night and finish the next steps the following day.

Next, you’ll be creating the tacky base for the chrome to stick to. While you can use any quick dry top coat here, Jackie recommends a 3-free (meaning it’s free of toluene, formaldehyde, and dibutyl phthalate) formula for ease. “ Toluene can sometimes cause certain nail polish colors to ‘bleed’ or ‘run’ so this just helps to avoid any unnecessary cleanup,” she says.

Again, you’ll need to let this layer dry until you don’t get any dents if you press into it. Luckily, this usually takes less than five minutes with a quick dry. “I cannot emphasize enough the importance of letting each layer dry,” Jackie says. “Most problems that come up (polish wrinkling, bubbles, weird texture, chrome looking cloudy etc.) are because one of the layers wasn't fully dry.”

Finally, the fun part. Jackie uses her finger to apply the chrome (but only on regular polish—doing so on gel could lead to skin irritation) but you can also use an eyeshadow sponge or cosmetic wedge.

To apply, tap your tool of choice into the chrome powder pot and gently wipe it onto the nail. Then, use the tool to buff the chrome into your nail until it creates a smooth, metallic finish. Once you’re happy with how it looks, use a big fluffy brush to brush away the excess.

Once the chrome is applied, wait at least another 15 minutes to ensure the top coat underneath it is truly, 100% dry. Then, Jackie says to swipe a 240 grit buffer block along the free edge of your nail to remove any excess chrome powder. “Now, brush off any additional excess and use a clean cosmetic wedge to gently wipe and tap over the nail to get any leftover loose chrome,” she says.

After lots of trial and error, Jackie found that the alcohol-based top coat DRK Seal Glaze “keeps the mirror shine and stays on nails longer than a water-based top coat.” You’ll want to apply two coats of it at this point, waiting about 10 minutes (or until the cloudiness turns clear) in between each coat. Make sure you’re applying thin coats that go all the way to the edges of your nails—if you miss any spots, those areas could turn glittery. Then, wait at least 30 minutes for the glaze to dry completely.

After everything is completely dry, you’ll want to finish with the same top coat you applied before the chrome powder. Even on gel polish chrome is notoriously prone to chipping, but Jackie says this glaze + top coat combo helps with longevity, especially if you use the top coat to wrap the tip of your nail.

Unlike with gels, removal is way easier than applying here: Jackie says you can take this off just like you would any other regular polish manicure.

If all of this is making your head spin, Estrada notes that there are some chrome-effect regular polishes that can get pretty close to this mirror-like finish. “A chrome-effect lacquer is an alternative finish, not a promise that every bottle will reproduce the same mirror effect as a professional pigment-and-gel system,” he says. “The important thing is to choose the right product for the finish you want, rather than expect an improvised combination to perform identically.” Sally Hansen and Essie, both sell chrome-effect polishes, though they do look more glittery than a true chrome finish. There’s no shame in this game, Estrada says: “There’s nothing wrong with taking the more straightforward route.”

Whats Up Nails Hideaway Ridge Filler Base Coat Nail Polish

L.A. Girl Cosmetics Glossy in a Flash Quick Dry Top Coat

Kara McGrath is a contributing beauty editor at Cosmo with over 15 years of experience reporting on beauty. She’s covered nail art and nail trends extensively, plus is an amateur nail artist in her spare time. For this story, she spoke with nail artist Jackie of @jackie.shades and professional nail artist Max Estrada.

Kara McGrath is a writer and editor with over 17 years of experience covering the beauty industry, women’s health, and a wide range of other lifestyle and wellness topics. After most recently serving as content director at Allure (2019-2025) and executive editor at Bustle (2014-2019), she is now fully freelance. In addition to Cosmo, her writing has appeared in Allure, The New York Times, Marie Claire, Business of Fashion, Playboy, NH Magazine, and more. Kara lives in New Hampshire with her dog, four cats, and a rotating cast of foster animals. You can find more of her work at karamcgrath.com.

Headquarters: Remote

Cadence is the clinical AI company automating the treatment of chronic disease. Grounded in partnerships with more than 20 leading health systems, deep EMR integrations, dedicated medical group, and experience serving a population of more than 100,000 patients, the system learns from real-world care delivery, improving as it scales. Cadence has been recognized by TIME as one of the Top 100 HealthTech Companies and by LinkedIn as a Top Startup (#4, 2025).

The Float Pool Registered Nurse supports Cadence patients by monitoring the alert board, responding to urgent clinical needs, and providing flexible coverage across care delivery workflows. This role requires a highly adaptable RN who can move seamlessly between alert monitoring, triage, and care management support to ensure continuity of care for patients with chronic conditions.

  • Active Compact Multi-State RN license
  • Active RN licensure in one or more of the following states: CA, NY, or HI

Schedule Requirements:

What you need

  • Active RN compact multi-state license.
  • Associate Degree in Nursing.
  • 5+ years of clinical nursing experience in outpatient, inpatient, chronic care, telehealth, or related settings.
  • Strong experience with clinical triage, including telephone or virtual triage.
  • Critical care or emergency nursing experience strongly preferred.
  • Experience supporting Chronic Care Management programs preferred.
  • Strong clinical judgment and ability to work independently.
  • Ability to monitor patient vitals, symptoms, and labs to identify care needs.
  • Ability to follow up with patients to gather clinical information and triage appropriately.
  • Excellent verbal, written, and interpersonal communication skills.
  • Comfort working across multiple systems and technologies.
  • Passion for delivering high-quality, patient-centered care.
  • Adaptability and flexibility in a fast-paced, remote environment.

We are looking for a Product Builder to join the IDP & RPA pod. You will take real problems from customers, decide what is worth solving, and build and ship the solution yourself, using AI tools to get from idea to production quickly without lowering the bar.

Product Building is how Camunda's Experience organization builds the applications people use on top of Camunda 8, such as Hub, Operate, and ProcessOS. Small pods of builders own a product area from customer problem to shipped change, with no hand-offs between product management, design, and engineering. We are setting this model up now, so you will help shape how it works.

The IDP & RPA pod builds how Camunda processes work with documents and legacy applications. With intelligent document processing (IDP), customers create extraction and classification templates in Camunda Hub and publish them so a process can use AI to turn invoices and other documents into structured data. With robotic process automation (RPA), customers integrate legacy applications that have no APIs, using Robot Framework scripts on a standalone RPA worker. The hard problems come with putting LLMs into customer processes: extraction quality, model and prompt choices, testing and trust, and working for AI agents as well as for people.

Help shape product direction for IDP & RPA by combining customer input, usage data, and field feedback into clear priorities.

Partner with the other builders and product strategy to turn problems into shipped features, using short written proposals and prototypes to align early.

Make judgment calls with incomplete information within your scope and own the outcome.

Communicate trade-offs and decisions clearly to stakeholders who don't live in the details the way you do.

Use AI tools across the whole loop, from specs to prototypes to production-ready code, and hold a high bar for what you ship by reviewing and improving the output rather than accepting it.

Build, test, and ship improvements to the IDP & RPA experience, reading errors and tracing issues far enough to give your pod useful context.

Talk to customers and to support and customer success colleagues about how IDP is used and where it breaks, and turn what you learn into decisions about what to build next.

Pick up customer incidents and trace them across Hub, the connectors, and the deployed process of the customer.

Track what you ship against real outcomes and adjust when the data tells you to.

#7114. Grafana Labs: Engineering Manager - Grafana Infrastructure | USA | Remote

Grafana Labs is the company behind Grafana Cloud, the fully managed observability platform trusted by more than 10,000 organizations to ensure reliability, resolve incidents faster, and optimize telemetry at scale. Built on open source and open standards and designed for interoperability across any stack, Grafana Cloud brings AI to observability and observability to AI, giving teams (and their agents) unified visibility so they can see, understand, and act on all their disparate data, wherever it lives, and move at the speed of their ambitions. Customers, including Anthropic, Bloomberg, NVIDIA, Microsoft, and Salesforce, rely on Grafana Labs. We are a 100% remote company with team members across 40+ countries, backed by leading investors including Lightspeed Venture Partners, Sequoia Capital, GIC, Coatue, J.P. Morgan, CapitalG, and Lead Edge Capital. Learn more at grafana.com and follow us on LinkedIn and X.

We’re scaling fast and staying true to what makes us different: an open-source legacy, a global collaborative culture, and a passion for meaningful work. Our team thrives in an innovation-driven environment where transparency, autonomy, and trust fuel everything we do.

You may not meet every requirement, and that’s okay. If this role excites you, we’d love you to raise your hand for what could be a truly career-defining opportunity.

This is a remote position and we're considering candidates in the US or Canada (residents of Québec are not eligible for this position).

Grafana Cloud moves millions of metrics, log lines, and traces per second from our customers' environments into a highly available, low-latency stack that processes and stores this data, and serves it to dashboards and alerting tools. We aim to grow this to hundreds of millions per second, and it's critical that as we grow, we improve our performance, increase our reliability, and do it efficiently and securely.

The Foundations department owns the Internal Engineering Platform (IEP): the Kubernetes clusters, cloud service provider resources (CSP), hardware, identity, delivery, and observability systems that Grafana Labs's application engineers use to build, deploy, and run their workloads.

We're hiring an engineering manager for the Foundations Infrastructure group, which owns the cloud infrastructure Grafana Cloud runs on. The group provisions and operates Kubernetes clusters and CSP resources across GCP, AWS, and Azure; enforces identity, access, and security controls across those environments; and runs the observability and cost-visibility systems that keep the infrastructure measurable and accountable.

You'll lead one of the squads of platform engineers that comprise the group.

We're platform engineers, and our application teams are our users. Our job is to give them leverage: self-service building blocks that take care of the hard parts of infrastructure, so they can focus on what they're building. We treat the platform as a product and value the experience of the people who use it. When a change is well communicated and understood, teams welcome it, and it lands as an improvement that makes the right way the easy way. You'll lead a squad that makes that happen.

Foundations is a global team that works asynchronously. The people who own a component or problem make the decisions about it, once stakeholders have had a chance to weigh in. Application teams build and run their own services; we give them a self-service platform to do it on.

Every engineer is on-call for the components their squad provides. We plan on-call, tactical work, and time off into every quarter, so roadmap work is roughly half of an engineer's time rather than all of it.

#7108. 6th Circuit upholds jury win for Ohio State in former graduate student’s sexual harassment lawsuit

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

While the plaintiff argued the district court incorrectly instructed the jury, the 6th Circuit said she’d failed to raise these objections at the proper time.

A former Ohio State University graduate student could not convince a federal appeals court to overturn a jury’s verdict in favor of the school in her sexual harassment lawsuit because she failed to show that her trial was unfairly prejudiced, according to a Tuesday decision.

The plaintiff in Huang v. The Ohio State University alleged that a faculty advisor subjected her to unwanted touching and that, when she resisted his advances, he retaliated by manipulating her candidacy exam for an engineering doctorate. She brought claims under Title VII of the 1964 Civil Rights Act as well as the 14th Amendment’s due process right to bodily integrity.

A district granted summary judgment for Ohio State on the plaintiff’s Title VII claims, holding that she was not an employee of the university within the law’s definition and therefore could not bring those claims. At trial, a jury held for the plaintiff’s advisor. She appealed to the 6th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals, which reversed and ordered a new trial.

At the second trial, the jury determined that the plaintiff was not an employee because Ohio State and the advisor did not have the “right to control the manner and means by which [the plaintiff] accomplished her work.” The jury also ruled in favor of the advisor on the plaintiff’s 14th Amendment claim.

The plaintiff again appealed to the 6th Circuit, alleging that the district court incorrectly instructed the jury on what facts were relevant to its determination of whether she was an employee. She also claimed that the court abused its discretion in excluding certain evidence and that it improperly prohibited her from requesting economic damages in her closing argument.

Each of these claims failed, the 6th Circuit held.

The plaintiff forfeited her challenge to the court’s jury instructions because she failed to raise it, despite the district court specifically asking whether the parties had objections or edits to its instructions. To grant a third trial on this point “would be unfair to the court and the defendants,” the 6th Circuit said.

In her evidentiary argument, the plaintiff claimed that the district court improperly prevented her from submitting portions of a slideshow containing the results of an Ohio State campus climate survey on sexual assault. She argued that the presentation would have made her allegations more plausible.

However, the plaintiff failed to include the relevant slides in her appeal, leaving the 6th Circuit unable to conclude whether they had sufficient weight to affect the jury’s verdict. The appeals court also could not determine whether the slides offered anything “above and beyond” other testimony in the case.

In all, the plaintiff failed to show that any of her allegations amounted to unfair prejudice warranting a new trial.

The case represents one in a handful of recent jury decisions to favor employers facing Title VII lawsuits. In March, a jury held for Haribo of America in a former employee’s discrimination and retaliation case, determining that the plaintiff’s dismissal was a mutually agreed-upon separation between the parties rather than retaliation.

And in a 2025 case, a jury sided with Levi Strauss in a lawsuit alleging that a former executive did not receive a promotion because of her pregnancy, gender and age.

#7105. This $120 Dyson Airwrap Dupe Works Even Better on Fine, Thin Hair

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Admittedly, when the Dyson Airwrap came out, I wasn’t entirely convinced it was meant for my hair type. I watched all the reviews: People with long, luscious hair achieve, well, long, luscious hair. My bob haircut (and fine strands) could never get hairstyles like that. Then, I became a beauty editor with access to all the fancy beauty devices, and I finally tried it. Within weeks, it replaced all of my hair tools. I could smooth my ends, give myself a ‘90s blowout, add waves, dry my hair quickly—all with one device. And it’s significantly less damaging too.

But since it was released, a zillion Dyson Airwrap dupes have come out, offering up similar results for a fraction of the price. Because that’s the catch: The Airwrap is great, but it’s a whopping $650 ($500 if you get it on sale right now for Black Friday). My curiosity is piqued every time a new multi-styler launches, but my go-to favorite for the past few years is the Ella Bella 6-in-1 Professional Hot Air Styler. And while it’s 20% off for Black Friday (at the lowest price I’ve seen all year), I’m breaking down exactly why it’s my favorite for fine, short hair.

With six attachments, this all-in-one air styling tool was made to replace virtually every other device in your cabinets. It features a hair dryer, two rotating curling irons (one for each side of your head), and three hair dryer brushes (one oval, one round, and one paddle for straightening). Like the Dyson Airwrap, this device uses hot air, not heated plates, to style, which helps reduce heat damage. The airflow on this thing is pretty damn powerful, reaching 110,000 RPM. And there are three temperature modes and two speed levels to help you tailor your styling to your hair type and needs (typically, the thicker your hair, the higher the temperature should be; and the same goes for speed). The device also boasts a “noise-reduction design,” so it sounds a little quieter than others.

My hair is truly as baby-fine as it comes and dries naturally straight. For this reason, it’s usually very soft and silky (love), but refuses to hold a style for longer than an hour or so (hate). I also color my hair (nope, not a natural redhead), so it’s damaged and prone to breakage. I’m growing out my bob haircut right now, so it’s a little past my clavicle—à la the clavi cut—and I have bangs that always require heat styling to look good. Currently, my go-to styles are typically a smooth blowout that makes my ends look less stringy in the bob grow-out period, or soft waves that add movement and texture. All this to say: The Ella Bella Styler has a lot of work to do (sorry!).

I received an initial rendering of this tool nearly a year ago, and it quickly became my go-to Dyson Airwrap dupe. In fact, I keep it at my desk at the office to style my hair after morning workout classes. The brand just released this slightly upgraded version that I hadn’t used yet, and upon first impression, it looks significantly higher quality. When I opened the carrying case and felt the tool, it’s lightweight (aka easy to hold for a long time if you are working with much more hair than I am) but looks luxe. The packaging is

Totally. At under $150 (a true fraction of the cost of the original Airwrap), the Ella Bella 6-in-1 tool is a pretty solid option if you’re looking to spend less money but still get a high-quality tool. I especially love how it works on my short, fine hair, but I could see someone with long hair falling in love with this too. I’ve already stashed mine in the green room at the office for midday touch-ups, and two people have asked if they could try it.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with more than seven years of experience researching, writing, and editing hair stories.

#7102. Alabama lawmaker eyes 3-year bachelor’s degrees for public colleges

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The forthcoming proposal would allow the state’s universities and community colleges to create reduced-credit programs in high-demand fields.

Orr framed his proposal as a way to help affordability and meet workforce demands.

“Alabama higher education institutions need to be more innovative and accommodating to meet the needs of students in the 21st century,” he said on social media Saturday.

The Commission on Colleges and Universities, the accreditor that oversees most public colleges in Alabama, has already paved the way for three-year degrees in the state. In March, it approved a formal pathway to allow for the creation of reduced-hour bachelor’s degrees with 90 to 100 required credits.

Traditional bachelor’s degrees typically require at least 120 credits.

“We want institutions to have the flexibility to design programs that help students reach their goals efficiently while ensuring that the degrees they earn continue to reflect the quality and credibility that accreditation represents,” Stephen Pruitt, the accreditor's president, said at the time.

Though much of the discussion around accelerated bachelor’s degrees is theoretical, the model has gained increasing popularity in the last few years.

In early 2023, no U.S. colleges offered three-year reduced-credit bachelor's degrees , according to the nonprofit Jobs for the Future. Since then, some 100 such programs have been announced or launched.

Some states have already approved such programs.

A 2025 Iowa law directed the state’s three public universities to create three-year bachelor's degrees as a means of addressing workforce demands and decreasing the cost of college attendance. The state board of regents signed off on two such program proposals this month, pending regulatory approval.

One is an online bachelor’s in applied professional studies at the University of North Iowa, expected to launch next fall.

Deirdre Heistad, the university’s interim associate provost for academic affairs, said in a Nov. 12. statement that the new program will serve adults with some college credit but no credentials, many of whom “work as educational support professionals, service coordinators or in other fields where a bachelor’s degree is increasingly expected at the next level.

At least two private institutions in Iowa, Coe College and Upper Iowa University, already offer an accelerated bachelor’s program.

And in September, the Oklahoma State Regents for Higher Education changed its policies to allow the creation of four new accelerated bachelor’s degrees at three public universities.

Other states are still weighing the concept.

This summer, the State Council of Higher Education for Virginia announced it would partner with Ohio higher ed officials to create a blueprint for 90-credit bachelor’s degrees. Both states have laws mandating that bachelor’s degrees from their public colleges require at least 120 credits.

#7100. Information Security Engineer - Application Security at Camunda

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

Hi there! We're hiring an Information Security Engineer with a focus on Application Security to join our Information Security team at Camunda.

We are a team that takes security seriously without making it feel like a locked door. We work in a transparent, straightforward way, and we care about doing the work right rather than just checking a compliance box.

You will work side by side with our R&D engineers: reviewing designs, threat modeling new features, digging into code and helping teams make good, risk-based decisions about the vulnerabilities that come in. You'll also help build the internal tools that make this work scale, including our CVE registry and the LLM-assisted workflows we use to analyse code and triage findings.

Camunda is an AI-first company, and InfoSec is no exception: AI agents are part of how we review code, assess vulnerabilities, and build our tools. You will help shape how that works.

This is a hands-on role for someone who already has solid AppSec foundations and wants to grow. We are a generalist team, so you will also take part in our on-call rotation and incident response, and get exposure to other areas of security. But most of your time will be spent with engineers and building and maintaining internal tools and processes.

Vulnerability management: triage findings from SCA, container and code scanning, external reports and internal security testing; assess whether Camunda is actually affected; agree fix / mitigate / accept with engineering and follow through to release.

Threat modeling and security reviews: run threat modeling sessions and design reviews with R&D teams for new features and architecture changes, and turn the results into concrete, prioritised actions.

Code analysis with LLMs: use and improve LLM-assisted tooling (agents, prompts, reusable skills) to review code, validate findings and reproduce issues, while applying your own judgement to know when the model is wrong.

Build internal security tooling: contribute to the CVE registry and related automation that connects scanners, engineering teams, and customer communication. Much of it is built on Camunda itself.

Help engineers do the secure thing by default: answer questions, review pull requests, improve guidelines and occasionally run a session or workshop.

On-call and incident response: share the InfoSec on-call rotation with the other engineers and help investigate and resolve security incidents.

Support the wider team: help write security advisories for our customers, answer customer security questions and support audits.

Around 3+ years in security or software engineering, with at least 2 of them working on application or product security (AppSec, product security, pentesting, or a security-focused developer role all count).

Experience at a SaaS or software product company, working closely with engineering teams that ship often through modern CI/CD and cloud environments.

Hands-on vulnerability triage: you've worked with tools like Snyk, Trivy, Semgrep, CodeQL or similar, can tell a real issue from a false positive, and can explain how exploitable something actually is in context (CVSS, exploitability, configuration).

Threat modeling and design review experience (e.g. STRIDE) on real systems, ideally web apps, APIs, or distributed services.

You read and write code. You're comfortable reading Java (Camunda is built on it) well enough to follow a data flow and spot a security bug. You can write scripts and small tools and contribute to an existing codebase with review.

You build with AI, not just chat with it. You use AI coding agents (Claude Code, Cursor, Copilot or similar) in your daily work to write tools, analyse code or automate tasks. And you know their output still needs a second look, especially when it comes to security.

Clear, friendly communication with engineers. You see yourself as an enabler, not a gatekeeper, and you can explain risk to people who aren't security specialists.

Some Python and/or JavaScript experience, or experience building internal tools.

Willingness to learn and use Camunda, since we build a lot of our own security automation on it. Prior BPMN / workflow experience is a plus.

Experience responding to security incidents or being part of an on-call rotation.

Exposure to security advisories, CVE disclosure or bug bounty programmes.

Knowledge of AI/LLM security risks (OWASP Top 10 for LLMs / agentic apps, prompt injection, tool abuse).

Experience securing Kubernetes or container-based workloads, or open-source / B2B software.

#7097. Federal student lending caps are ‘blunt instrument’ to prevent defaults, researchers say

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A “richer set of criteria,” including program earnings and credit profiles, would better address that risk, according to a new paper from the Federal Reserve Bank of Philadelphia.

The Republican spending bill enacted in 2025 ended the Grad PLUS lending program and set hard lifetime caps on federal student lending at $100,000 for graduate students and $200,000 for professional students — a classification being disputed in court.

Republicans aimed to reduce student debt loads through the law. When it passed, Rep. Tim Walberg, the Republican chair of the House Education and Workforce Committee, said the loan overhaul would help ensure “students don’t borrow excessive debt they can never repay.”

To examine the potential impact of the caps, the Philadelphia Fed research looked at three-year default rates among borrowers who took out federal graduate student loans and entered repayment between 2011 and 2016.

The overall default rate for those borrowing over the new limits — in inflation-adjusted dollars pegged to 2025 — of 6.5% for graduate students compares to a 3.3% rate for those borrowing below the limits. Master’s students drove up that rate, as they defaulted 7.2% of the time when borrowing above loan cap levels.

But the size of students’ federal loans wasn’t the only factor that predicted defaults.

Another was borrowers’ credit risk profiles. Three-year default rates for all graduate borrowers declined as their credit scores improved, the Philadelphia Fed found.

Borrowers with credit scores between 300 and 579 — deemed “deep subprime” — defaulted at a 12.4% rate. That rate declined until it reached just half a percent for those with scores between 770 to 850, the highest credit scores.

In every credit score bracket, students who borrowed above the new limits defaulted more often than those who borrowed less than the capped levels.

But those credit profiles don’t tell the full story, the researchers noted. The earnings potential of students’ fields of study also played a major role in defaults. Sub-prime and near-prime borrowers who studied in the highest-earning programs defaulted less frequently than less-risky prime borrowers in lower-earning programs.

“Loan limits are a far coarser signal of loan performance than either creditworthiness or program characteristics,” the researchers wrote.

But under the new caps, riskier student borrowers could be locked out of higher-earning programs by a private lending market that is expected to take over a larger share of student lending.

Following the Democratic sweep of the midterm elections, the federal loan caps could come under political pressure. Rep. Bobby Scott, the ranking Democratic member of the House education committee, has blasted the federal loan caps.

In March, for example, he and other Democrats on the committee said that the lending structure will “further restrict higher education access, particularly for low- and middle-income borrowers” and “destabilizes our higher education system and harms students.”

However, any changes to the new lending system in the near term would require sign-off by President Donald Trump or enough Republicans joining with Democrats to overturn a veto.
