#7093. Court approves $5.4M bankruptcy sale of Bacone College assets

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An environmentally focused real estate firm is buying the property and other assets of the Native American-serving institution that shuttered last year.

Along with Bacone’s campus, the sale includes two parcels of land that together total about 67 acres. It also includes a trove of books, archival materials, paintings and other artworks, many of them with significance to Bacone’s history and Native American communities.

ODP and its CEO, Reed Oppenheimer, did not immediately respond to questions Tuesday about the firm’s plans for the Bacone property and other assets.

The real estate firm has made efforts to get certain “significant buildings” of Bacone’s onto the National Historic Registry, according to court testimony recounted in the judge’s order.

The property also has a small cemetery. Oppenheimer must provide “reasonable access to those wishing to visit a grave site,” the judge wrote.

The order approving the sale came over the objection of a former Bacone professor who raised numerous concerns, including that the Muscogee Nation might have a claim to the land under a 19th century treaty. The judge wrote that testimony and documents provided to the court confirmed the legal transfer of land that ultimately came under Bacone’s ownership.

Bacone — originally named Indian University — began teaching classes in a Baptist mission house in 1880.

In the early 20th century, the institution deepened its focus on Native American education, recruiting Native staff and faculty and developing classes in Native American culture. In the 1930s, Bacone established an arts center that developed a distinct aesthetic and expressive movement.

By the 21st century, some faculty members questioned the college’s commitment to Native American students and faculty. In its last years, only a handful of faculty were Native American, and its Native student makeup fluctuated widely, dipping to as low as about one-third in 2018.

In fall 2023, Bacone had just 106 students, less than a tenth of the 1,184 students it enrolled in 2010. By February 2024, The Associated Press reported that the college was teetering on the edge of closure amid lawsuits, debts and the risk of accreditation loss. When visiting the campus, a reporter for the news service found buildings in disrepair, molding Native works of art and accusations that administrators had embezzled funds.

When Bacone initially filed for bankruptcy, leaders intended to keep the college open. But after a federal bankruptcy trustee found evidence of financial mismanagement, a judge ordered the college into Chapter 7 bankruptcy. That kicked off a court-managed liquidation of what was, until its closure, the oldest continuously operating college in Oklahoma.

#7088. Syracuse University adds AI review to undergraduate admissions

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The technology will conduct the first review of applicants’ transcripts, while the final stages will “remain entirely human,” a senior official said.

Syracuse is one of the latest and most prominent examples of colleges turning to AI in the admissions process. The private university will use AI for “synthesizing grades into a uniform scale, gathering information on the student’s high school and drafting a short summary to attach to the application.” The admissions team will continue review all applications.

“To be clear, AI does not admit or deny any student,” Denny Nicholson, Syracuse’s dean of admissions, said in a statement. “Every decision is made by a member of the admissions team, and later stages of review, including admitting the class, remain entirely human.”

Applicant data is stored securely and is not used to train AI models, the university said.

Syracuse expects AI to free up admissions counselors to work directly with applicants and to speed up the decision-making timeline.

“These tools augment the work of our admissions team; they do not automate it,” Jeff Rubin, senior vice president and chief digital officer at Syracuse, said in a statement. “We have tested them carefully, and we will continue to evaluate them to ensure they serve our team and the students we serve.”

Syracuse is facing a projected budget deficit and is reviewing its executive and administrative roles to rightsize its workforce.

While Syracuse did not cite reduced cost as a motivating factor for AI use, higher education and other sectors have turned to automation to trim budgets and conserve resources.

Some colleges that use AI in admissions have also addressed how applicants can use the technology.

In September, the Georgia Institute of Technology announced that it would incorporate AI into its admissions process at several “pain points,” as described by one senior official.

The technology now assists admissions officials in reviewing transcripts and credit transfers, cutting the latter process to “a fraction of the time.”

On its website, Georgia Tech calls AI a potentially “powerful and valuable” tool for applicants and released guidance for its acceptable use.

Prospective students can use AI “to brainstorm, edit, and refine” ideas but “should not copy and paste content” they didn’t create themselves into their applications, Georgia Tech said in a statement.

The institution also said AI can be a “helpful collaborator” for students who don’t have any other sources of assistance in the application process.

The University of Pennsylvania provides guidance on AI for its notoriously selective admissions process.

Applicants can use tools, including AI, to organize their thoughts and check grammar without violating the university’s policies. But “submitting work, ideas, or experiences that aren't authentically” theirs is disqualifying, the Ivy League institution said.

“Here’s a simple test: if you couldn't explain, in an interview or conversation, how you arrived at what you wrote, or speak to it in your own words, it isn't ready to submit,” the university’s guidance said.

On the same webpage, Penn said it uses AI in admissions for “administrative rather than evaluative” processes. They include confirming an application is complete and accurate, “ensuring consistency of the understanding and interpretation of transcript and grading scales,” reviewing transfer credits and assisting with documentation processing. Penn staff members review those results before they affect students’ files, it said.

Like Syracuse, Georgia Tech and Penn said admissions decisions remain in the hands of humans.

But not all colleges are incorporating AI into admissions.

At a conference last month, admissions officials from the University of Michigan, Rice University, and the University of Georgia said their institutions do not use AI to review applications.

Rice’s executive director of admission, Charles Murphy, also expressed concern about trying to use AI to detect AI in students’ applications.

His team enters Rice’s essay prompts into generative AI to get a baseline of what an AI response could look like, he said. But he’s wary of the detection software’s likelihood to flag false positives.

“I’ll speak for myself, not the university. But my thinking is, even if it’s 90% right, every 10 times it’s wrong,” Murphy said. “Is that really good enough?”

#7082. DOL flips independent contractor switch back with new final rule

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Secretary of Labor Keith Sonderling said the agency’s renewed “economic reality” test would provide greater clarity to both workers and employers.

The rule marks a return, of sorts, to the first Trump administration’s 2021 independent contractor final rule. But DOL Wage and Hour Administrator Andrew Rogers said Thursday that the new regulations add “modifications and enhancements” in an effort to create a “streamlined” worker classification analysis.

Those changes include a clarification that the agency’s test applies to the FLSA as well as the Family and Medical Leave Act and the Migrant and Seasonal Agricultural Worker Protection Act, since both use the same definition of “employ” that the FLSA does, per DOL. But the test doesn’t alter assessments under other federal laws, like the Internal Revenue Code or the National Labor Relations Act, Rogers said.

DOL also included 14 separate “fact-specific” examples that illustrate how the rule would be applied to different occupations, such as delivery workers and truck drivers.

The agency named three “less probative” factors that would only be considered in its analysis to the extent that they show whether a given worker is in business for themselves, Rogers said. Those factors include:

Rogers noted that, with respect to any of the factors, the actual practices of the worker and potential employer are more relevant than what is contractually or theoretically possible.

“While courts will have the ultimate say, the final rule and its illustrative examples should aid employers — particularly small employers — in making decisions regarding the proper classification of workers,” James Plunkett, shareholder at Ogletree Deakins, said in an email to HR Dive.

The final rule is effective 60 days from the date of publication in the Federal Register.

#7079. Engineering Manager - Pipelines at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We're looking for an Engineering Manager to lead the Pipelines team at Supabase. Supabase Pipelines is a managed change data capture product. It copies the tables you select from Supabase Postgres to other systems, then streams later changes. The product is in public alpha, with BigQuery, ClickHouse, DuckLake and Snowflake destinations.

Pipelines uses Postgres logical replication and Supabase ETL, our open source replication engine written in Rust. The team works close to the database. That means replication slots, WAL retention, initial table syncs, and the load they place on a customer's source database. The team also builds and runs the destination connectors. Correctness, ordering and recovery matter there as much as speed.

The Engineering Manager is a people, delivery and technical role. We don't expect you to write production code. We do expect you to join design discussions and help set the bar for engineering quality, testing and operations.

Build a healthy, high-trust team culture in a remote-first, globally distributed environment.

#7078. How colleges and schools can grow career-focused dual enrollment

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Career and technical education and dual enrollment are “powerful levers” together, said a researcher at the Community College Research Center.

At DeKalb High School and New Boston High School in northeastern Texas, students in healthcare courses use mannequins to practice caring for patients, while students in welding classes fabricate real parts.

They are participating in dual-enrollment programs developed with Texarkana College that are designed to help high school students make progress toward a postsecondary credential before they graduate.

The community college works with school districts to train high school instructors to teach the college coursework. Texarkana also sends academic coaches to both high schools to help students chart a post-high school plan in their chosen field.

The partnership between the high schools and the community college serves as an example of what an October report from the Community College Research Center calls an "unprecedented opportunity” to do more to pair dual enrollment with career-focused education.

The report estimates that more than 1 million of the nation’s 3.8 million annual high school graduates are taking three or more high school credits of career and technical education, or CTE, but not taking any dual-credit courses.

Sarah Griffin, research associate at CCRC and lead author of the new paper, said that matters because 2025 research from the center found that Texas students with a CTE focus were more likely to enroll in college and complete a degree when they took dual enrollment courses in high school than those who did not.

There was also an economic payoff. By age 24, the prior research found, the CTE students who took dual enrollment classes were earning 16% more than CTE students who did not.

“CTE and dual enrollment together are really powerful levers for students because they are facilitating exploration of careers and college programs for students,” Griffin told Higher Ed Dive.

“Because they're so widespread, they're already serving large numbers of students across the country,” Griffin said. “Therefore, they're really practical for practitioners to be able to leverage.”

Shaun Dougherty, professor of education and policy at Boston College and a former senior adviser at the U.S. Department of Education, said the report's call for a greater focus on career-connected dual enrollment is “fairly aligned with current policy” at the state level.

Dougherty pointed to Colorado's Concurrent Enrollment Programs Act and Kentucky's Dual Credit Scholarship as examples of states providing a favorable policy environment for combining dual enrollment with CTE. The Colorado law covers tuition for dual-enrollment students, while the Kentucky program helps high school students in the state pay for dual credit classes at participating colleges.

“While there has been pushback on four-year college-going as a policy priority, career-aligned programs that span secondary and postsecondary levels and that focus on preparation for relevant skills have gotten far more focus,” said Dougherty.

Griffin said it’s paramount for those looking to establish career-connected CTE programs to develop a vision of the optimal dual enrollment experience for CTE students and how it will help them find their way after high school — whether that means going to a community college, a four-year college or a technical program that’s aligned with a specific job.

Griffin recommended a CCRC-developed framework called Dual Enrollment Equity Pathways for those looking to boost dual enrollment for CTE students.

Pursuing these practices to scale up dual enrollment for CTE students will pay off in the long run, Griffin said. The strategy is a practical approach for helping students earn a postsecondary credential and increasing the likelihood “they'll be able to secure a good-paying job by the age of 30,” she said.

Senior leaders attributed its financial woes to “shifting enrollment trends, unpredictable state and federal funding, and rising costs.”

The congressional chamber could vote this week on the Protect College Sports Act, a proposal intended to stabilize the shifting collegiate landscape.

#7071. How Richmond, Virginia, schools are tackling a $680M facilities funding shortfall

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Voters approved a 1% sales tax to help with funding. In an assessment, each school’s needs are weighed against what replacement would cost.

As it became clear in early November that Richmond voters would pass a 1% sales tax measure for school construction and renovation, Richmond Public Schools Superintendent Jason Kamras was grateful that residents would approve a tax increase even while households around the country are experiencing an affordability crisis.

“We are dealing with decades of deferred maintenance [and] disinvestment,” Kamras told Facilities Dive. “Our challenges are legion, which is why I think the voters so overwhelmingly supported the 1%.”

The district faces a roughly $680 million, 20-year backlog in maintenance, repair and upgrades to a portfolio of more than 40 schools, according to an independent assessment the agency conducted in 2024. Almost a quarter of the schools are more than 100 years old. About 70% are more than 50 years old. Only a handful are less than 15 years old.

Earlier this month, voters in Richmond were asked to decide whether to increase the sales tax by 1% to create a fund exclusively to build new public schools and renovate existing facilities. When the final votes were counted, 68% of the electorate voted in favor of the assessment, according to an Axios Richmond report.

Kamras says the actual need is even more than what the assessment shows — “well north of $1 billion,” he said.

With passage of the 1% assessment, the district could raise an estimated $850 million over the next 20 years. But that’s still not enough to meet all of the needs, Kamras said.

“I wish the sales tax would solve everything,” he said. “It's going to take us a lot farther, a lot faster, but it by itself does not solve all of our facilities' needs.”

The district currently gets about $2.5 million a year for facilities work, according to the school.

Adding to its challenges, the district fired its director of facilities earlier this year for improper use of funds and as of mid-November was operating with an interim director, according to the district’s website. The former facilities chief is alleged to have made personal purchases using his school district credit card, according to news reports.

“The investigation found direct evidence of asset misappropriation and unprofessional conduct” by the official, a school district investigation found, ABC 8 News reported in October.

Among the needs the district faces is a $938,000 roof repair at Woodville Elementary School, $2.5 million for brick wall repairs at John Marshall High School and $1.7 million for updated windows at Thomas Jefferson High School.

Mold, flood damage and HVAC issues are widespread, according to the assessment, which used a facility condition index to determine what each of the school’s needs are weighed against what it would cost to replace the school.

“The Facility Condition Index … is defined as the ratio of the cost of current needs divided by the current replacement value (CRV) of the facility,” the assessment says.

Schools that score between 0 and 5 are considered new or well-maintained, with little or no visual evidence of wear or deficiencies. Those with a score of between 5 and 10 show wear but are still in a serviceable and functioning condition. Those with a score of between 10 and 30 show signs of hard or long-term wear and are nearing the end of their useful or serviceable life. Those with a score of 30 and above have reached the end of their useful or serviceable life, making “renewal” necessary.

The Richmond City Council will need to pass an ordinance to enshrine the sales tax increase into law. The district has been using funds from a local meals tax to patch together funds for school construction dating back to 2018.

Kamras said the city will determine which projects to prioritize based on feedback from nine stakeholder meetings, as well as building conditions, enrollment trends, and concentration of student need.

Richmond is not alone in its need for school renovations. School districts on average only receive about 17% of their construction and equipment funding from states, according to 21st Century School Fund’s State of our Schools report. The federal government doesn’t help much, either, adding just 3.2% of the funds schools need, the report says. Local communities tend to be left to foot the bill.

The result is that U.S. public schools face an $85 billion shortfall on facilities, the report found.

Mary Filardo, executive director of the 21st Century School Fund, would like to see the states and federal government do more to support school facilities across the country, she told Facilities Dive. She said the country should view its ailing school infrastructure similar to debt.

“You can not replace that leaky roof, but it's not going away. You still owe that building a roof,” she said.

Filardo said that schools’ HVAC systems are complicated. They’re not just for temperature control, but also air filtration and dehumidification. Retrofitting older schools also presents unique challenges.

“In the 1950s, there would be one outlet in a classroom, maybe,” she said. “If you were lucky, one on each wall.”

From the states’ perspectives, Filardo sees a wide range of approaches to start addressing needs. Wyoming, for example, has used coal lease bonuses — mining company payments for extraction permits — and Massachusetts has implemented a statewide sales tax.

Meanwhile, in Virginia, Richmond was one of many seeking to boost facilities budgets. The same measure failed to pass in 32 of the 47 localities statewide.

Kristen Nye, executive director of Virginians for Modern Schools, an organization supporting the measure, said voter education proved to be very important in Richmond’s case. She added that the measure opened up opportunities that may have previously seemed out of reach.

“Let's say those plans haven't been touched in well over a decade,” Nye said. “They're sort of blowing the dust off of them and taking them back out in the community again.”

Richmond City Councilor Katherine Jordan led the city’s legislation that put the sales tax measure on the ballot.

“Very often education is a popular talking point on the campaign trail, but when it comes to budget time, somehow it just doesn't make it through,” she said.

Richmond School Board Member Katie Ricard said that if the measure had not passed, it might have been put back on the ballot for a future year. RPS might also have looked to the state for more funding.

Although the measure passed, the school district will need additional funding, said Ricard, noting that she expects the district to continue applying for grants.

“We have 42 school buildings across the district,” Ricard said. “It just gets really costly to maintain all of them. They are old, and their systems are pretty much hanging on by a paperclip, sometimes until they fail.”

#7062. Legal Assistant at Atticus

At any given time, 16 million Americans are experiencing a crisis that requires urgent help from our legal system or government. The right assistance could transform their lives. But today, most never get it.

Atticus makes it easy for any sick or injured American to get life-changing aid. Our mission is to tear down barriers between people in crisis and the aid they deserve. In the last six years, we’ve become the leading platform connecting people with disabilities to government benefits. We also help victims of accidents, misconduct, and violence get compensation from insurance.

So far, we’ve helped hundreds of thousands of people access over $10 billion in life-changing aid —and earned over 20,000 five-star reviews. And we’re just getting started.

We've raised more than $100 million from top VC firms like Forerunner, Google Ventures, and True Ventures, and we’re on our way to creating a category defining business assisting Americans in need.

Each day, thousands of people seek our help getting disability benefits or insurance compensation. They’re intimidated by the process, unsure where to turn, and desperate for good advice.

As a Legal Assistant at Atticus, your job is to ensure they get and stay on the right path. This job is different from that of a traditional legal assistant. The job mixes responsibilities in any good legal assistant’s comfort zone (build client relationships; support paralegals and attorneys in filing and managing claims) with those unique to being an operator in a fast-growing tech company (help Marketing source clients who’d be great in testimonials, or user test new tools for Product).

In a typical week you might do any of the following:

Make contact with a client that’s in danger of slipping through the cracks at SSA, and help get their case back on track.

Work with a client’s team to run down missing paperwork at SSA.

Take on an inefficient workflow that’s slowing the team down (e.g., processing case updates), scope an improvement, get team buy-in, and execute the improvement.

This role is a rare opportunity to join a fast-growing Series C startup and do meaningful social impact work. It’s designed to be a path to building expertise in the fields of law you support and taking on greater responsibilities over time (e.g. becoming a paralegal).

You have a Bachelor’s degree from a four-year university with strong academic performance.

You have experience in a professional role that required a substantial amount of customer-facing work and/or administrative tasks.

You are a resourceful team-player ready to dive into a scrappy startup environment where you will learn quickly and get your hands dirty.

You are comfortable in a variety of interpersonal conversations; you can be empathetic and kind when speaking with people in a moment of crisis, and can be thoughtful and organized when interacting with attorneys or the SSA.

You have a demonstrated commitment to doing good and helping people.

You have professional experience helping others apply for government benefits.

You have experience at a startup or other fast-paced, agile environment.

We are strongly committed to building a diverse team. If you’re from a background that’s underrepresented in tech, we’d love to meet you.

This is a rare opportunity to join a startup that has strong traction (substantial funding, well-respected backers, tremendous growth, and many happy customers) but is still small enough that you can have a huge impact and play a role in shaping our culture.

We’re a certified B Corporation tackling a critical social problem. Our mission to help people in need drives everything we do, and your work here will touch many lives.

We offer competitive pay — including equity for every employee — and generous benefits:

Medical and dental insurance with 100% of employees and their families premiums covered

15 vacation days & ~20 paid holidays each year (including two weeks at the end-of-year)

$1,000/year reimbursable stipend for education and training outside of work

Up to $1,200/year student loan repayment assistance

Humble, thoughtful, smart, fun colleagues

We anticipate the base salary band for this role will be between $50,000 and $75,000 in addition to equity and benefits. The salary at offer will be determined by a number of factors such as candidate’s experience, knowledge, skills and abilities, as well as internal equity among our team.

This job is fully remote and we’re committed to empowering everyone with flexibility. Work remotely, and travel to LA (on the company dime) as needed to be with your colleagues – usually quarterly, plus offsites. We care a lot about building a great culture and we think some interactions need to happen in person, so we put a lot of thought into retreats, offsites, and other ways to gather.

#7058. Public Policy Lead - US at Kindred

Kindred is a members-only home swapping platform that unlocks the ability to live a travel-rich lifestyle through the power of community. By exchanging primary residences with trusted peers, renters and owners alike can match with Kindred spirits and explore new destinations without breaking the bank.

Kindred is revolutionizing the “third option” of travel accommodation, compared to short-term rentals and hotels. Since launching in 2022, Kindred has grown to a global community of 300,000 members across 150+ cities and puts home swapping on the map as a radically more affordable, responsible, and connected way to travel.

To support that momentum, Kindred has raised over $150M in capital from some of the most credible investors in Silicon Valley, including Index Ventures, Andreessen Horowitz, New Enterprise Associates, Bessemer Venture Partners, Caffeinated Capital, Elad Gil, and founders of companies like Opendoor, Figma, ClassPass, and more.

Kindred’s co-founders are proven leaders from the early team at Opendoor ($15B+ exit), who have each built and scaled products that today generate $1B+ annual revenue combined.

We’re looking for the world’s top builders, executors, and believers to help us make travel more meaningful.

You can learn more about us in Forbes and The New York Times.

This is Kindred’s first dedicated public policy hire. You’ll join a three-person legal and policy team (reporting to the General Counsel) and own the execution of Kindred’s public policy and government relations efforts, with a focus on North American markets but covering additional markets as necessary.

The role is primarily focused on proactive advocacy to shape the policy environment for home-swapping. You’ll build relationships with elected officials, regulators, and industry groups. You’ll commission and manage economic impact research. You’ll develop city-level partnerships that normalize home-swapping as a category distinct from other travel categories. You’ll also develop working familiarity with the tax, housing, and accommodation regulations that intersect with Kindred’s model, and help parse how those frameworks apply (or don’t) to credit-based home-swapping. You’ll do all of this initially as a team of one, with outside consultants to support you and a path to building a team as the company grows and the function matures.

Execute Kindred’s public policy strategy with an emphasis on proactive advocacy.

Build and maintain relationships with elected officials, regulators, and staff at the local, state/provincial, and national levels. Represent Kindred in meetings, hearings, coalition convenings, and industry events.

Track proposed housing, short-term rental, tax, and platform regulations that could impact Kindred’s model, identifying where Kindred’s credit-based, non-commercial structure creates meaningful regulatory distinctions. Use AI-assisted tools and other technology to monitor developments across jurisdictions.

Further develop and evolve Kindred’s core policy narrative and positioning materials. Draft position papers, comment letters, testimony, and advocacy materials.

Propose and negotiate city-level partnerships that promote and normalize home-swapping. Build relationships with housing advocacy organizations, tourism boards, trade associations, and aligned industry coalitions.

Commission and manage third-party economic analyses on the impact of Kindred’s model, collaborating with internal business teams and outside consulting firms.

Identify, evaluate, and manage outside consultants cost-effectively (lobbyists, policy advisors, research firms).

Partner with internal teams to develop externally facing materials and data packages for stakeholder engagements.

Coordinate with the General Counsel on lobbying compliance, political activity disclosures, and regulatory filings.

7–10 years of experience in public policy, with at least 3 years at a technology company or similar fast paced environment.

Experience building or meaningfully contributing to building a public policy or government relations function, not just operating within an established one.

Track record of productive relationships with elected officials, regulators, and policy stakeholders.

Experience managing outside consultants, lobbyists, or policy advisors across multiple jurisdictions.

Strong written and verbal communication skills, including experience drafting policy position papers, advocacy materials, or regulatory comments.

Experience with policy matters at the city and state level in the United States, with an interest in policy work in new jurisdictions.

Fluency with AI tools and a willingness to integrate them into policy monitoring, research, and content development workflows.

Willingness to operate as an individual contributor while building the function, with the judgment to know when to engage outside support.

Experience in or adjacent to the sharing economy, home-sharing, travel, housing, or short-term rental sectors.

Experience at a growth-stage company (under 500 employees).

Familiarity with vacation/short-term rental, housing, and land use regulations, as well as related tax regulations.

Comfort with detailed regulatory analysis: ability to read and interpret statutes, ordinances, and administrative rules, and translate their implications into actionable policy positions. A law degree and legal practice experience is a plus but not required.

Build something new: You’ll help define the function and shape how Kindred engages with governments and regulators from the ground up.

A genuinely different model: Kindred’s credit-based, non-commercial home-swapping model is structurally distinct from existing travel lodging categories, contributing to sustainable tourism objectives.

Path to leadership: As the company and function grow, this role offers the opportunity to greater leadership.

Competitive compensation: Base plus meaningful equity in a growth-stage company.

At Kindred, we know that good things happen when we look out for one another. We offer a thoughtful, human-first benefits package designed to support health, wellbeing, and connection.

Our openings may span more than one career level. The base salary offered depends on factors such as experience, transferable skills, business needs, impact, and market data.

Flexible time off, plus 12 paid holidays per year

21 credits per year to stay in Kindred homes

$4,000 annual travel stipend to help offset travel-related costs

Remote-first and remote-flexible work environment, with opportunities to work from anywhere and gather in person, including at Kindred HQ in San Francisco

Regular team and company offsites to connect, collaborate, and build together

A vibrant, inclusive, and highly skilled team that ferociously protects team chemistry

A culture of high standards, feedback, and continual growth

We are committed to providing equal employment opportunities for all applicants and employees. Kindred does not discriminate on the basis of any protected characteristic, including race, color, ancestry, national origin, religion, creed, age, disability, sex, gender, sexual orientation, gender identity, gender expression, medical condition, genetic information, family care or medical leave status, marital status, domestic partner status, military and veteran status, or any other characteristic protected by US federal, state or local laws, or the laws of the country or jurisdiction where you work. Pursuant to the San Francisco Fair Chance Ordinance, we will consider for employment qualified applicants with arrest and conviction records.

#7055. Rethinking employability skills in K–12 education

What does it actually mean to prepare students for the future? What skills do they need to succeed in tomorrow’s workforce?

→ Baca artikel asli

#7054. 87% of educators use personal devices in teaching. The next great 1:1 investment might be the one at the front of the room.

Even in districts that issue laptops to staff, there is a good chance a teacher’s personal laptop, tablet or phone is carrying some of the workload. In fact, a recent study by the University of Arizona found that 87% of educators surveyed use their personal devices for at least some teaching-related tasks. Those personal devices were purchased, set up and managed by themselves, with two-thirds reporting that they download institutional documents (for example student data, grade books) onto them.

While districts are providing teaching devices, these numbers suggest that alongside the school-issued laptop, a share of teaching work, from grade books to student records, ends up on devices the district cannot see or manage.

In the movement around enabling technology in classrooms for students, this study presents a larger conversation on how technology is being used by educators who are working closest to them.

Over the last two decades, classroom devices have gone from shared computer labs to one-to-one deployment. Today, 96% of teachers say their students use learning devices in class. This did not happen overnight. It took years of planning from administrators, careful execution by IT teams, significant investment and district grants. As a result, student devices have a tried-and-true playbook. Districts know what to expect with refresh cycles, IT teams understand device management and superintendents can anticipate their budgets and funding for new devices.

Teachers are the cornerstone of classroom tech, and it is time their devices got a playbook too. As they regularly carry heavy computing loads, in everything from running lessons, online grading, to joining calls, they deserve the same level of detail and detail to device deployment and management. This is more handing out a laptop. It means giving teachers a device capable enough that they don’t need to reach for their own.

This is even more important as teachers regularly handle sensitive data. As the study found, more than half of participants who downloaded institutional documents onto personal devices, reported that their devices had automatic cloud backups through personal accounts.

While using a personal device for teaching might seem harmless in nature, the device is not under the districts' scope. Updates, encryption and sign-in guidelines are not under the control of the district, rather, this responsibility falls to the teacher.

This is alarming as education remains the fourth most targeted sector for cyber-attacks. Robust student, teacher and parent data combined with increased digitalization and lack of cybersecurity resources have left devices and information vulnerable to malicious attacks.

Paired with the right training on cybersecurity best-practices, a well-managed district-deployed device overseen by IT teams helps to enable schools to secure sensitive student data. These devices deploy continuous updates, encourage fast and secure sign-in and ensure private information stays secure.

The ASUS P5 featuring the Intel® Core™ Ultra 7 Series 3 Processor, is a strong example of a device that can be managed at scale with the performance to multitask through a full teaching day. Engineered to seamlessly juggle grade books, lesson plans and video calls, Intel processors deliver all-day battery life and built-in hardware-based security that helps protect student data — that’s the power of Intel Inside®. For IT teams, the device supports centralized management tools that let them deploy updates, monitor device health and enforce policies across a district’s fleet.

The planning, budgeting and IT expertise that has put a device in nearly every student’s hands in the United States is ready to extend to teachers. With the right devices, they can do even more and districts can protect the data that matters most. The next great 1:1 investment might be the one at the front of the room.

Attend Get-To-Know-You (GTKY) meetings with marketing, sales, Solutions Consultants, RevOps, and Creator Success. Get access to our CRM, call recordings, and product analytics, and start listening to recent sales calls. Understand how product marketing currently works at Kit

First Month: Shadow 15+ sales calls and talk with 10 established creators, including recent wins, losses, and churned accounts. Audit the sales content reps use today and how product usage currently routes accounts to sales. Set baselines with RevOps for win rate, sales cycle, deal size, and expansion revenue. Share a written point of view with the entire growth team on who our established creator buyer is and the three biggest gaps in how we sell to them. And an action plan on how we can win these creators.

First Year: Sales runs on messaging and tools you built, pitch with confidence and we drive 2X inbound MQLs from when you started. You’ll own and lead our channel strategy, setting the vision, driving cross-functional alignment, and partnering with Marketing to bring it to life. You'll run a quarterly win/loss program and share what you learn openly, build a clear path for sales leads on our website, and make sure launches that matter to larger accounts ship with both a self-serve and a sales-assisted version. Your work has a direct impact on helping Kit reach $100,000,000 ARR while protecting the self-serve engine that got us here.

Segmentation and buyer definition: turning firmographic and product usage data into a clear picture of who to sell to and when

Messaging and positioning for a sales-assisted buyer, including competitive differentiation and objection handling

Sales enablement that reps actually use: demo narratives, discovery guides, business cases, and battlecards

Writing and storytelling that turns product depth into a business case a non-technical buyer can act on

Working with CRM, product analytics, and pipeline data to measure funnel conversion, win rate, and deal size

Customer and market/competitor research, including win/loss interviews and call analysis

Systems thinking: connecting people, processes, data, and tools to identify opportunities and build repeatable solutions across the go-to-market motion

Strong AI fluency: knowing when to leverage AI and how to build AI-powered workflows or tools that scale product marketing work, with human judgment and quality checks built in

Pricing and packaging input for a new tier or add-on (nice to have)

7+ years in B2B SaaS product marketing, including at least 2 years supporting a sales team selling to mid-market and/or larger accounts

Added a sales-led motion on top of a product-led one at a high-growth company, and can show what changed before and after with clear metrics

Built a deal toolkit and enablement program from little or nothing, and tied it to a sales metric like win rate or deal size

Ran a win/loss or customer research program that changed how a team sold or built

Ran an expansion or account-based program for an existing customer base (nice to have)

Worked in email, martech, or the creator economy

You lead without a formal title. You align sales, product, RevOps, and finance on goals, trade-offs, and what success looks like, then keep things moving.

You advocate for creators in decisions, and you protect the self-serve experience instead of pushing every account toward sales.

You act with urgency and stay focused on hitting OKRs and owning outcomes.

You work in public. You share findings, drafts, and the reasoning behind decisions so others can build on them.

You connect data to decisions, and you help others get sharper about using it.

You raise the bar on quality and iterate toward it, and you shape how your team uses AI, from call analysis to keeping battlecards current.

Experience in only self-serve or only enterprise sales. This role lives in the space between them, and you'll need to understand both.

Treating enablement as a deck handoff. The work includes coaching reps, measuring what gets used, and revising what doesn't land.

Wanting a sales playbook already in place. Much of this motion doesn't exist yet, and you'll be building it as you learn.

Preferring to be measured on output rather than outcomes. You'll share accountability with Sales for win rate, deal size, and expansion revenue.

Needing extensive approval before acting. You'll be trusted to make calls on speed versus quality and to explain your reasoning.

Discomfort with direct, candid feedback, especially from sellers on what's working in live deals.

#7050. Building an executive business case for unified college and career readiness infrastructure

As states and school districts deepen their commitment to college and career readiness, there is increased focus on accountability. Complex state graduation requirements, workforce readiness indicators and reporting mandates increasingly require districts to provide evidence that proves their students are actually progressing toward post-secondary success.

While accountability matters, practitioners understand that policies and metrics alone do not move the needle. Educators need the bandwidth and quality instructional programming to guide learners across their K–12 journeys. Students themselves need authentic, engaging opportunities to explore pathways, connect with caring adults and take ownership of their futures.

Meeting these expectations demands a coherent system that aligns central district priorities, educator capacity and day-to-day student experiences. Building the executive case for that shift requires leaders to demonstrate how unified CCR infrastructure can deliver a meaningful return.

In many states across the country, districts are required to demonstrate that students are completing required readiness activities, updating graduation plans and meeting statutory benchmarks across every campus. Managing each requirement—from career readiness indicators to financial literacy and work-based learning—as an isolated program creates administrative friction and disparate student records that need to be reconciled for reporting.

Adopting a comprehensive system changes that dynamic. In a national study conducted by Hanover Research, 84% of surveyed district leaders using a unified platform reported that it successfully supports multiple district requirements and core workflows. An identical 84% reported an enhanced capability to fulfill reporting requirements, while 70% cited a reduction in time spent on manual administrative tasks like logging lessons and tracking transcripts.

A coordinated strategy delivered through a centralized platform gives district leaders the infrastructure to satisfy complex statutory demands without juggling separate data sources and disjointed spreadsheets.

Centralizing and simplifying workflows reduces administrative burden, giving educators additional bandwidth for personalized student guidance.

A cohesive and collaborative district-wide readiness strategy relies on educators having easy, reliable visibility into student progress. When tools and data operate in silos, counselors and teachers spend precious advising time tracking down files or working with fragmented information, rather than working directly with students.

A connected system reclaims that time. The Hanover study found that educators using a unified platform experienced an estimated 33% reduction in administrative workloads tied to readiness workflows. Crucially, 54% reported improved efficiency in student advising. When routine clerical tasks are automated, counselors enter conversations with multi-year context in hand, turning transactional compliance check-ins into meaningful, high-value guidance.

Connecting workflows cuts down operational drag and restores educator capacity for direct student mentorship.

District investments deliver the most meaningful returns when they nurture self-directed learners, rather than usher students through rote tasks to complete checklists.

When planning is embedded as a continuous developmental journey, instead of an 11th-hour graduation requirement, student engagement deepens. Among educators and district leaders surveyed by Hanover with a unified platform in place, 76% reported greater clarity in students’ post-secondary goals, 74% observed increased confidence in post-secondary planning, and 73% cited stronger student ownership over the entire process.

Giving educators a shared, full K-12, cross-department platform creates an unbroken continuum of support, encouraging students to explore options, recognize their strengths and actively shape their life after high school.

Most districts already invest in a variety of programs to address compliance, staff needs and career exploration. Breakdowns occur because these efforts operate in isolated silos.

Separate CCR initiatives and tools creates redundant work for staff, disconnected data erases momentum during middle-to-high-school transitions, and fragmented reporting blinds district leadership to participation gaps across buildings.

Delivering a unified district-wide CCR program requires education, communication, collaboration and planning. A unified infrastructure helps enable a coherent program by connecting existing efforts, so every localized investment contributes to a larger strategic framework.

Unified infrastructure doesn’t replace the work districts are already doing. It connects that work so each investment contributes to a broader strategy.

Building an executive business case requires evaluating readiness systems beyond traditional accounting metrics. The Hanover report establishes a Return on Impact framework that measures value across four interconnected dimensions:

Hanover’s findings reinforce the organizational value of a unified system: 74% of respondents reported stronger accountability across grade spans, while 73% pointed to enhanced support for strategic planning and data-informed decision-making.

To translate these findings into practical leadership discussions, district decision makers can anchor evaluations around four questions:

District leaders do not have to choose between operational efficiency, state compliance and student success; a well-designed CCR strategy advances all three. Sustainable readiness infrastructure centers on what a school community can achieve when compliance mandates, staff capacity and student exploration are linked through one coordinated strategy.

To learn how a unified platform transforms CCR strategy into long-term impact, visit Xello.world.

Kit's most established creators run real companies, with teams, large lists, dedicated sending needs, and a buying process that our self-serve motion wasn't built to serve. #7034. School specialist’s time spent on IEPs after hours may count toward FMLA eligibility

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The finding comes in a recent U.S. Department of Labor opinion letter involving a salaried occupational therapist.

DOL’s Nov. 17 FMLA opinion letter could affect how school districts and other employers determine whether an employee is eligible for leave.

Generally, employees are eligible for leave for a serious health condition or other covered reason if they work for an employer that is subject to the law for at least 12 months, have at least 1,250 hours of service with the employer during the 12 months before their FMLA leave starts, and work at a location where the employer has at least 50 employees within 75 miles.

Without accurate timekeeping, it’s up to the employer to demonstrate that an employee does not qualify for FMLA leave, DOL said. And if that lack of recordkeeping is because the employee is exempt from the Fair Labor Standards Act’s recordkeeping requirements for minimum wage and overtime purposes, “eligibility for FMLA leave is presumed for any employee who has been employed for at least 12 months,” according to FMLA regulations.

FMLA requires employers to provide up to 12 workweeks of unpaid, job-protected leave annually for qualifying family and medical reasons.

For the occupational therapist in question, DOL said, their after-hours tasks must be counted as hours of service. And while the agency said it didn’t have enough information to determine that the individual’s travel time must definitively count toward their service hours, it appeared likely to be the case.

#7023. Head of Research at CoinMarketCap

CoinMarketCap is hiring a Head of Research to turn CMC data into research that institutions, media and partners trust and pay for. This is a senior, hands-on role reporting to the CMO.

CMC is the most referenced price and market data source in crypto. Our research already reaches millions each month through reports like our Monthly Exchange Report, Asset Watch and Market Pulse, and is cited by Bloomberg, WSJ, Forbes and Fortune. The next step is different. We are building CMC into a data platform for crypto, tokenized assets and broader markets, and research is how we prove the quality of that data to the people who buy it.

You will own our research agenda end to end: what we publish, how rigorous it is, and how it supports our enterprise data business. You will also be the senior voice on sensitive market analysis for CMC and its partners.

Set and run the research roadmap, from recurring flagships (monthly exchange report, weekly market and asset coverage) to deep dives on exchanges, tokenized assets, RWAs and market structure.

Build institutional grade research that showcases CMC data to banks, funds, data buyers and enterprise API clients, working closely with our API and BD teams on what buyers need.

Own methodology and data integrity: define how we measure volume, liquidity, flows and reserves, document it, and make sure every published number holds up to scrutiny.

Lead research on sensitive or fast moving market events, balancing speed, neutrality and accuracy, and handle review requests from partners with sound judgement.

Be a credible public face for CMC research with tier one media, on panels and in interviews.

Manage and develop a small research team, currently one research analyst, and use AI and automation to scale output without lowering quality.

Partner with marketing, editorial and product to turn research into formats people actually read and share, from long form reports to charts and short social posts.

7+ years in research or data analysis, with at least 3 in crypto or digital asset markets.

A track record of published, data heavy research that institutions or serious media rely on.

Deep understanding of market structure: exchanges, spot and derivatives, liquidity, onchain flows and proof of reserves.

Strong quantitative skills and hands on data work (SQL or Python, APIs, large datasets).

Excellent written English and the judgement to present sensitive findings neutrally and accurately.

Experience leading or mentoring analysts.

Background at a crypto data provider, an institutional research desk or a TradFi data business.

Understanding of how data is packaged and sold to institutions (licensing, enterprise feeds, research subscriptions).

Knowledge of tokenized assets, RWAs or traditional market data.

Existing relationships with tier one financial and crypto media.

Comfort using AI tools to automate data pulls and drafts.

Mandarin, given our close work with Chinese speaking partners and media.

#7021. Districts put off tough decisions in face of enrollment declines

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

As of 2024-25, post-COVID enrollment declined but staffing and the number of operating schools increased, Stanford researchers found.

The study does not include national data from the 2025-26 and 2026-27 school years, when school districts have finalized more school closure and consolidation decisions.

Still, the Stanford researchers said the misalignment between enrollment, the number of operating schools and staffing seen so far since the pandemic is likely due to the local political environments in which districts are making their decisions.

“The prospect of school closures and layoffs constitutes a highly visible and consequential threat to parents and teachers who can readily lobby school-board members and district leadership,” the study authors said. “That pressure, coupled with the often brief and provisional nature of superintendent tenures, can forestall confronting difficult decisions.”

A separate report released Tuesday by the Reason Foundation, a libertarian think tank, found that among nine of the nation’s 12 largest public school systems, there were almost 938,000 empty seats during the 2025-26 school year. Those nine districts, on average, only filled about 71% of their student capacity.

The district with the most underutilized capacity was the Los Angeles Unified School District, which had 365,410 empty seats with a 49% utilization rate, according to the Reason Foundation.

LAUSD leadership has hinted that closures and consolidations could be considered in the near future given the district’s drastic enrollment declines over the past two decades.

This fall, LAUSD Superintendent Andrés Chait addressed the district’s enrollment trends in a video shared with school community members. Chait noted that enrollment is nearly half of what it used to be 20 years ago. Even so, he said, LAUSD currently operates almost the same number of school campuses.

Similar to the Stanford analysis, the Reason Foundation report acknowledged that school closures can be difficult decisions for students and local communities. “But public school enrollment declines and districts’ financial situations are unlikely to improve, so these challenges must be addressed,” the Reason report said.

If districts cannot properly “right-size” the number of schools they operate, the Stanford analysis said, consequential spending cuts will occur elsewhere, such as building maintenance or support services for student mental health and instruction. And if districts continue to struggle with properly managing their finances, they may be more likely to face a state takeover, the Stanford researchers said.

#7019. AI Native Head Of Growth - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

Most growth teams call themselves data-driven. They have a dashboard, an agency and a ChatGPT licence to write ad copy faster. That’s the 99%.

We want to be part of the 0.1%: a company where AI doesn’t just help the growth team, it runs the growth loop. Every day, on every channel.

Then it’s your chance to define a new category : The Operating System (OS) for Human Greatness Augmented with AI.

Science-backed. Design-led. AI-powered. Global.

We’re not looking for someone who only advises on what to test, we’re looking for someone who builds it. A hands-on Head of Growth who can ideate, write the copy, design the strategy and launch experiments directly.

1. Generate hypotheses. Read the signals (ad performance, webinar attendance, sales calls, objections, churn reasons, comments, competitors’ ads) and propose what to test next, with a cost and an expected impact.

2. Act across every tool. Not a chatbot in a tab: it reads and writes in our whole stack, from ad accounts to CRM, landing pages, webinars, email, social DMs and analytics.

3. Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, offers, voice, member stories, past campaigns and what they taught us, in one shared company brain.

4. Ship its hypotheses. Produce the hook, the ad cut, the page, the email sequence or the funnel variant, and put it live.

5. Measure with real KPIs. Every test is tied to a number that matters (cost per qualified lead, show-up rate, close rate, CAC, payback, LTV), not to likes or reach.

6. Learn and loop on its own. Scale what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and launch the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

This is already real. Our growth hub tracks every hypothesis and experiment, with its result. An ads library of thousands of creatives feeds our creative research. Agents cut ads from long-form content, prepare webinars, wire Instagram comment-to-DM automations and audit our tracking after every live. Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day.

We believe self-help is broken: generic theory, a quick high, no real change.

We believe people deserve more. More than content. More than motivation.

They deserve a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges, and grows with them.

Growth at Paradox means bringing people into something that actually changes their life, and proving it.

- Human Intelligence (coaches, peers, and mentors)

- Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

- Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

To create one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world.

Here’s the bet: a company that learns by itself grows in a way a company of people alone can’t. A small growth team running a self-learning machine can out-test, out-learn and out-grow teams a hundred times its size.

We hold ourselves to the standards of Apple, Spotify and MasterClass. While others hide their numbers, we let our results speak.

As Head of Growth, you’ll be the architect of a growth machine that learns by itself. In a classic growth team, people do the work and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the work, you design the system, set the bar and make the calls.

You don’t just manage a team, you get your hands dirty to identify growth levers and pull them yourself.

❌ The old way: a Head of Growth hires people and agencies, briefs them, and checks their work.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: your team is mostly agents, and you build them yourself. You write their skills, plug them into our tools (ad accounts, CRM, webinars, email, analytics) and write the evals that tell an agent what "good" means: a hook that stops the scroll, a page that converts, a test that’s worth its cost.

You give autonomy step by step: first an agent suggests, then it acts once you approve, then it acts alone within a budget and guardrails. You move each agent up a level only when its track record earns it.

Example: in your first month you build the agent that reviews every ad set overnight. At first it only suggests. By month three it pauses losers and moves budget by itself within limits you set, and you only see what was decided and why.

❌ The old way: a media buyer checks the dashboards each morning, changes bids by hand and waits for the weekly report.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build and improve the agents that watch every ad set, page, webinar and email sequence around the clock. They spot what’s dropping, find the cause, fix it or launch a variant, and report what they did.

You give autonomy step by step: an agent can pause an ad on its own long before it can raise a budget. You decide where each agent stops and a human takes over.

Example: show-up rate drops on Tuesday’s webinar. Before anyone notices, an agent has found the cause (a reminder that never went out), fixed it and started testing a new reminder sequence.

❌ The old way: a creative team briefs an agency, waits two weeks and gets five ads.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build the agents that turn every live, interview and member story into hooks, ad cuts and shorts, that study what the best advertisers in the world are running, and that write in our voice.

Your taste becomes the eval: you write down what makes a great hook, a great cut and a great story, in rules and examples the agents check themselves against. Each time you reject something, the reason goes back into the rules.

Example: one webinar becomes dozens of hook variants by the next morning. You keep the best few, and next week the agents’ first drafts are already closer to your bar.

❌ The old way: an analyst rebuilds the numbers in a spreadsheet at the end of the month, and nobody trusts them.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build the agents that connect every touch to cash, from ad to lead, live, sale and transformed member, and that audit the tracking after every campaign.

No number, no test: you define the KPIs that matter (CAC, show-up, close rate, LTV, payback), and agents refuse to launch a test that isn’t wired to one.

Example: after each live, an agent cross-checks the ad platform, the CRM and our analytics, finds where they disagree and fixes the leak before the next live.

❌ The old way: lessons live in people’s heads and leave with them.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: every result goes back into the company brain, and agents read it before proposing anything. The machine never learns the same lesson twice.

You measure the machine itself: tests per week, time from idea to live, the share of the work done without a human, and how many agents moved up an autonomy level this month. You push those numbers up.

And always: no fake scarcity, no fake claims, real stories told with consent. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. You do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time building agents, writing evals and making calls, not doing the work by hand. Most experiments are generated and shipped by agents. The output grows every month while the headcount doesn’t.

You’ll own these KPIs: LTV / CAC ratio, funnel conversion rate by stage, experiments launched per week.

Desired outcomes: conversion rates 2x better than baseline, growth experimentation that runs continuously, a predictable growth engine.

Tools you’ll master: GA4, Optibase, Vidalytics, Clarity, Hotjar, Amplitude, Figma, Looker Studio, VWO, Optimizely, Claude Code, and whatever helps you see what others miss.

You have ideally a minimum of 3 years of experience in Conversion Rate Optimization, Growth Experimentation, or Performance Marketing, ideally within SaaS, e-commerce, or digital product environments.

You have a track record of designing, implementing, and scaling A/B and multivariate tests that have generated measurable improvements in revenue, user activation, or conversion.

You’ve built with AI and agents yourself: Claude Code, automations, APIs, evals, creative pipelines. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

A Conversion Architect, skilled at diagnosing funnel bottlenecks and translating data into structured experimentation roadmaps that deliver measurable business outcomes.

A Behavioral Strategist, fascinated by what drives human action, able to mix cognitive psychology, UX, and persuasion frameworks to design experiences that convert because they resonate.

A Data-driven Creator, fluent in GA4, Hotjar, AB Tasty, VWO, Amplitude, not just running tests, but connecting insights to revenue, retention, and lifetime value.

Loop thinker: you see every channel as a loop that can learn, and you can’t stop yourself from closing it.

Taste: you know a great hook in two seconds, and you’d rather ship one great ad than ten average ones.

Numbers-honest: you say when a test failed. You care about real revenue, not vanity metrics.

Builder, not manager: you’d rather build the system than coordinate an agency.

Growth over ego: you love hard feedback, you read, study and learn fast.

Cross-disciplinary: you move from psychology to spreadsheet to creative brief to code.

Analytical: you turn a vague problem into a clear hypothesis, a metric and an experiment.

English: Fluent English, you can hold a conversation on specific details with English-speaking colleagues.

Our chosen language for written communication is English.

❌ If you need clarity before chaos — wrong place.

❌ If you prefer balance over intensity — wrong season.

❌ If you build to deliver, not to elevate — wrong mission.

❌ If you want to optimize without getting your hands dirty in landing pages, copy, or design — wrong role.

❌ If you want to manage agencies and budgets rather than build a machine — wrong role.

✅ If you’re obsessed with psychology, systems and creative…

✅ If you want to build the growth machine that learns faster than anyone else…

✅ If you dream of using AI to bring transformation to millions of people…

Build one of the first truly self-learning growth machines, and write the playbook others will copy.

Work with filmmakers, psychologists, engineers, and creatives obsessed with impact.

You’ll compete with San Francisco companies.

-> 🏅 Being surrounded by A-players who strive to perform

-> Playing a crucial role in the development of a company whose content and products will be used by millions of people

#7016. Title IX sex bias employment case about to get its day in Supreme Court

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A lower court said institutions may not know when accepting federal funds that they are accepting liability under Title IX, when Title VII typically covers employment claims.

School districts could get a hint of how the U.S. Supreme Court will rule on a case that could change how teachers and other school employees bring sex discrimination claims when the justices hear oral arguments next week.

In Thomas Crowther v. Board of Regents of the University System of Georgia, the 11th U.S. Court of Appeals ruled in 2024 that Title IX’s right to sue doesn’t apply to employees, diverging from at least eight other appeals courts. At odds is whether school employees have the right to sue privately under Title IX outside of protections under Title VII.

Title IX protects anyone who interacts with a school — including students, employees and parents — from sex-based discrimination in federally funded education programs and is often invoked by students who are harassed, assaulted or bullied due to their sex. Title IX protections for school employees include, for instance, prohibiting discrimination against teachers due to pregnancy or pregnancy-related conditions, among other things.

Meanwhile, Title VII prohibits employment discrimination based on race, color, religion, sex and national origin.

In Crowther, two University System of Georgia employees — a basketball coach at Georgia Tech and an art professor at Augusta University — said the university system discriminated against them on the basis of sex when it fired them.

Georgia Tech women’s basketball coach MaChelle Joseph was fired in 2019 shortly after she filed an internal complaint that alleged the university was discriminating against her and her program by not providing the same resources as the men’s program.

At Augusta University, art professor Thomas Crowther was told his contract would not be renewed in 2021 after several students reported allegedly inappropriate conduct in his class — including sexual harassment — which triggered a Title IX investigation. That investigation process and ensuing negative performance reviews, he said, discriminated against him on the basis of him being male.

Joseph and Crowther both reported the alleged discrimination to the U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission, but also sued in the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of Georgia under Title IX.

The two cases were combined before the 11th Circuit, which said in its 2024 decision that schools may not understand when accepting federal funds that they accept potential liability for damages of Title IX claims by employees, when those types of claims are typically filed under Title VII.

The Association of Title IX Administrators called the Supreme Court case "one of the most consequential decisions impacting the intersection of Title IX and employment law in decades."

Multiple major education and legal organizations have filed briefs in the case, including the National Education Association and the National Women's Law Center.

"The American education system faces a severe and worsening crisis in educator recruitment and retention," said NEA's brief that was filed earlier this year in favor of the former University System of Georgia employees. "Anti-discrimination protections affect whether talented professionals enter, remain in, or depart from careers."

The NEA said that a court decision implicates "a nationwide educator workforce crisis, the professional environment in which recruitment and retention decisions are made, and ultimately the quality of education available to students across the country."

"When sex discrimination drives talented educators from the profession, the consequences extend beyond individual career harm and cause systemic degradation of educational quality," the teachers union said.

Granting teachers the right to sue privately under Title IX would help improve an environment where sex discrimination "has remained pervasive," the NWLC said in its own brief. The center cited teachers' high rates of sexual harassment from their colleagues and students, unequal pay for female teachers despite their overrepresentation in the profession, a lack of leadership opportunities, and pregnancy discrimination, such as a lack of accommodations and supportive school policies.

Such a decision would also help address lower investments in girls' school sports and barriers to STEM in education programs, NWLC said.

Title IX "can only be meaningful for women who work in education if they can enforce their right to be free from sex discrimination, including in court," the organization argued.

However, the Board of Regents of the University System of Georgia maintained in its opposing brief filed in October 2025 that Title IX does not include a private right to sue and that petitioners "had every chance to prove their claims for employment-based sex discrimination under Title VII."

"Had Congress desired that employees of educational institutions have a bonus remedy for employment discrimination under Title IX, unburdened by Title VII’s exhaustion requirements and damage caps, it could have provided as much," the respondents said. "It has not."

The case invokes another — Jackson v. Birmingham Board of Education, a 2005 Title IX case that was decided 5-4 in favor of the plaintiff.

That case was brought by Roderick Jackson, a high school girls’ basketball coach who spoke out to his supervisors after he noticed that his team was not receiving equal funding and equal access to athletic equipment and facilities. Following his complaints, he received negative work reviews and was ultimately removed from his position.

His lawsuit reached the high court, which decided that Title IX's private right to sue included retaliation claims for complaining about sex discrimination.

"Reporting incidents of discrimination is integral to Title IX enforcement and would be discouraged if retaliation against those who report went unpunished," the Supreme Court said in its decision. "Indeed, if retaliation were not prohibited, Title IX’s enforcement scheme would unravel.”

However, more than two decades later, Crowther will be heard by a much different bench, given the justices in the majority on the Jackson opinion have long since retired or died.

#7010. More students are homeless as districts face enrollment and budget losses

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A report from SchoolHouse Connection shows 1 in 32 students were identified as homeless, the highest share on record.

Between the 2018-19 and 2024-25 school years, the portion of students experiencing homelessness increased from 2.7% to 3.1%, with the overall population of students experiencing homelessness increasing 12%. During that same period, overall public school enrollment fell by about 1.3 million students.

Some of the largest increases in the share of homeless students were seen in states where homeless student identification increased alongside drops in overall public school enrollment. Those states include West Virginia, Montana, Arkansas, Hawaii, Maine and Vermont.

West Virginia had the largest share of homeless students compared to its overall public school population at 1 in 16.

Districts in that state have also closed around 140 since 2011, according to the West Virginia Center on Budget and Policy. Thirteen school closures have been proposed or approved in just the 2026-27 school year, according to the center's last update in mid-October.

Overall, the state has lost 3.5% of its students since last school year and 17% over the past decade, according to an October count by the West Virginia Department of Education.

"The drop in enrollment raises two concerns for students experiencing homelessness: tighter school budgets, and children who may not appear in school records at all," said the SchoolHouse Connection report about the issue nationally.

The budget squeeze, drop in overall enrollment, and increase in students identified as homeless come on the heels of federal pandemic relief funds drawing to an end in September 2024. The American Rescue Plan's Homeless Children and Youth funds provided a historic $800 million to identify and serve students experiencing homelessness. The additional funding allowed over half of U.S. school districts to access Education for Homeless Children and Youth funds.

In 2025-26, the first full year without ARP-HCY funds, many schools no longer had the staff and resources that helped them find these students, said SchoolHouse Connection. The report noted that the lost funding is a potential reason most states identified fewer homeless students despite a national increase in that population.

In fiscal year 2026, Congress appropriated only $129 million for the EHCY program, which means about 1 in 5 districts receive dedicated funding for homeless students. The EHCY program was established under the McKinney-Vento Homeless Assistance Act in 1987.

However, regardless of whether these funds are provided to districts, all districts are required under the McKinney-Vento Homeless Assistance Act to serve homeless students and remove barriers to their education.
